Synchronization with Annealed Disorder and Higher-Harmonic Interactions in Arbitrary Dimensions: When Two Dimensions Are Special
Rupak Majumder ID, Shamik Gupta ID

Abstract
The impact of disorder on collective phenomena depends crucially on whether it is quenched or annealed. In synchronization problems, quenched disorder in higher-dimensional Kuramoto models is known to produce unconventional dimensional effects, including a striking odd–even dichotomy: synchronization transitions are continuous in even dimensions and discontinuous in odd dimensions. By contrast, the impact of annealed disorder has received comparatively little attention. Here we study a D D -dimensional Kuramoto model with both fundamental and higher-harmonic interactions under annealed disorder, and develop an arbitrary-dimensional center-manifold framework to analyze the nonlinear dynamics near the onset of collective behavior. We show that annealed disorder fundamentally alters the role of dimensionality. With fundamental coupling alone, it completely removes the odd–even dichotomy, yielding continuous synchronization transitions with universal mean-field scaling in all dimensions. Higher-harmonic interactions preserve this universality while rendering the synchronization transition tunable between continuous and discontinuous. At the same time, they give rise to a novel, correlation-driven transition between a symmetry-protected incoherent phase and a symmetry-broken state lacking global synchronization, which is therefore invisible to the conventional Kuramoto order parameter. This transition is continuous in two dimensions but discontinuous in higher dimensions, revealing an emergent and previously-unrecognized special role of two dimensions.



Figure 1:
Phase diagram of the model is presented in (a) for
D=2
and in (b) for
D>2
in the
K_{1}
(strength of the fundamental interaction) –
K_{2}
(strength of the higher harmonic interaction) plane . The red dashed lines denote continuous transition and the blue solid lines denote discontinuous transition. The enclosed region by the blue solid and the red dashed lines and the
K_{1}
,
K_{2}
axes denote the parameter region in which the uniformly incoherent state (c), denoted by
\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{B}=0
, is linearly stable. The horizontal red dashed and the blue solid lines denote the transition points at which the order parameters
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{M}
shows a transition to a synchronized phase (d). The verticle red dashed /blue solid line denotes the transition points of the uniformly incoherent state to a symmetry broken state without global order (e) denoted by
\mathbb{P}=0
and
\mathbb{B}\neq 0
.

I
Introduction

Disorder plays a central role in shaping collective phenomena, with its consequences depending crucially on whether it is quenched or annealed. In systems with quenched disorder, impurities or random fields are frozen on the timescale of the dynamics, whereas in annealed disorder, the disorder variables fluctuate dynamically alongside the system’s intrinsic degrees of freedom. Quenched disorder is known to profoundly alter thermodynamic, critical, and dynamical behavior, influencing universality classes and even the existence and nature of ordered phases in both classical and quantum systems
47
;
5
;
39
;
95
. It can give rise to striking phenomena such as glassy dynamics
35
;
78
;
13
and non-self-averaging behavior
4
;
104
. By contrast, determining how annealed disorder influences collective phenomena has remained an enduring theoretical challenge
12
;
93
;
38
. The distinction between quenched and annealed disorder has thus emerged as a unifying theme across a wide range of contemporary research areas, including quantum gravity
7
, condensed matter physics
68
;
105
, active matter
21
, biophysics
64
, and sociophysics
50
;
75
.

Synchronization among interacting nonlinear oscillators provides a paradigmatic and broadly relevant setting in which both quenched and annealed disorder arise naturally
14
;
40
;
74
. As a prototypical form of collective order emerging from disorder, synchronization has been observed across an extraordinary range of systems, including biological
103
;
24
;
82
;
85
;
99
;
86
and chemical oscillators
106
;
53
;
91
, power grids
34
;
73
;
83
, quantum platforms
61
;
51
;
58
;
107
, and even financial systems
96
;
41
;
8
. Over the years, the Kuramoto model has emerged as a minimal and unifying theoretical framework for understanding the onset of synchronization in large populations of interacting non-linear oscillators
56
. The model describes phase-only oscillators with distributed natural frequencies that are globally coupled through the sine of their phase differences. Its analytical tractability has made it a cornerstone for the study of nonequilibrium phase transitions driven by interactions and disorder
88
;
90
;
1
;
76
;
36
;
43
;
81
;
42
;
15
;
57
. A central result is the emergence of a phase transition between incoherent and synchronized states at a finite coupling strength, whose nature depends sensitively on the form of disorder.

Recent work has shown, however, that even minimal generalizations of the Kuramoto model can give rise to qualitatively new phenomena. In particular, higher-dimensional extensions have been introduced in which each oscillator is represented by a
D
-dimensional unit vector rather than a single phase, with the conventional Kuramoto model corresponding to
D=2
. As argued in Ref.
19
, these models naturally arise in the context of flocking birds, fish schools, flying drones, opinion dynamics as well as in a surprising context of neutrino oscillations
77
;
45
;
79
. Analyses of these models in the presence of quenched disorder have revealed a striking odd–even dimensional dichotomy: synchronization transitions are continuous in even dimensions, while in odd dimensions, they become discontinuous and occur at vanishing coupling strength
19
;
108
;
37
;
20
. This unexpected result highlights the profound role of dimensionality in shaping collective dynamics
18
;
10
;
9
;
63
. Dimensional effects have since been explored in numerous extensions, including models with three-body interactions
46
;
25
;
55
;
52
;
65
, nonuniform
113
;
60
;
100
;
69
, adaptive
29
;
80
;
111
, and matrix-valued couplings
16
;
97
;
32
, among many others
112
;
98
;
62
;
28
;
70
;
48
. Notably, the overwhelming majority of these studies focus on quenched disorder, leaving open the fundamental question of whether dimensional dichotomies persist when disorder becomes annealed. In a related class of swarming models, annealed disorder has recently been studied both in the presence of quenched disorder in
D=3

109
and in its absence in arbitrary dimensions
110
.

A second, equally fundamental gap concerns the role of interaction structure. In two-dimensional synchronization models, higher-harmonic interactions are known to qualitatively modify phase transitions, alter critical behavior, and induce multistability
30
;
31
;
54
;
66
. Whether such effects persist, compete with, or fundamentally reshape dimensional phenomena in higher-dimensional systems remains unknown, particularly when higher harmonics and annealed disorder act simultaneously.

From a theoretical standpoint, a key challenge is development of analytical tools that go beyond linear stability analysis and are capable of addressing nonlinear dynamics in higher-dimensional synchronization models. In two dimensions, the Kuramoto model admits a powerful center-manifold framework
88
;
89
;
27
;
26
;
23
;
11
;
22
;
71
;
72
;
66
, which enables a systematic characterization of phase transitions beyond linear stability analysis. In higher dimensions and in the absence of annealed disorder, substantial progress has been achieved through generalized Ott–Antonsen-type ansatz
18
;
9
, allowing for exact low-dimensional reductions. However, such approaches do not extend naturally to systems with annealed disorder. Developing an analytical framework capable of addressing this gap is essential for understanding the nonlinear structure of the dynamics, determining the nature of phase transitions, and characterizing behavior near criticality.

In this work, we address these challenges by studying a
D
-dimensional Kuramoto model with both fundamental and higher-harmonic couplings in the presence of annealed disorder. The paper is organized as follows. In Sec.
II
, we summarize the main results. In Sec.
III
, we introduce the model and define the relevant dynamical observables; the symmetries of the system and their consequences are discussed in Sec.
III.2
. In Sec.
IV
, we formulate the dynamics in terms of a Fokker–Planck equation. A nontrivial
D
-dimensional generalization of the center-manifold reduction is developed in Sec.
V
. The resulting reduced evolution equations of the two order parameters and the associated phase transitions are presented in Secs.
V.4
and
V.5
. We conclude in Sec.
VI
. Technical details are provided in Appendices
A
–
P
.

II
Main Results

Motivated by the need to systematically characterize phase transitions in higher-dimensional Kuramoto models with both fundamental and higher-harmonic interactions in the presence of annealed disorder, we develop a nontrivial generalization of the two-dimensional Kuramoto center-manifold analysis to arbitrary dimension. This approach goes beyond linear stability theory and provides direct access to the nonlinear growth of instabilities near criticality, allowing for a rigorous determination of the nature of the associated phase transitions.

Our first main result concerns the fate of the odd–even dimensional dichotomy previously reported for higher-dimensional Kuramoto models with quenched disorder. We show that, in the presence of annealed disorder and fundamental coupling alone, this dichotomy disappears entirely. The transition from incoherence to synchronization is strictly continuous in all dimensions, and the synchronization order parameter exhibits universal mean-field scaling with critical exponent
\beta=1/2
, independent of dimensionality. Annealed disorder thus restores universality across dimensions, consistent with recent findings in related swarming models
109
;
110
. We further find that on introducing higher-harmonic interactions, the transition between incoherent and synchronized phases can be tuned from continuous to discontinuous in any dimension by varying the strength of such interactions up to a dimension-dependent critical value.

Our second main result is that higher-harmonic interactions give rise to a previously-unexplored novel transition from a symmetry-protected incoherent phase to a symmetry-broken state that does not exhibit global synchronization. Across this transition, the conventional synchronization order parameter remains identically zero and therefore fails to detect the ordering. Instead, the transition is characterized by a correlation matrix encoding ensemble-averaged correlations between different components of the oscillator vectors. This correlation-driven transition is continuous in two dimensions but becomes discontinuous in all higher dimensions, leading to a rich phase diagram summarized in Fig.
1
.

This dimensional dependence of the correlation-driven phase transition is the third main result of our work. Thus, in presence of higher-harmonic interactions, dimensional effects reemerge in a qualitatively new manner, making
D=2
uniquely distinguished from higher dimensions. At the nonlinear level, the special role of dimensionality is further reflected in the scaling of the leading nonlinear terms near the correlation-driven transition. In two dimensions, the instability is governed by a quadratic nonlinearity, whereas in higher dimensions, the leading contribution scales with a non-integer exponent equal to
3/2
. Taken together, these results identify
D=2
as uniquely special in the presence of higher-harmonic interactions, in sharp contrast to the fully universal behavior observed when such interactions are absent.

From a theoretical perspective, our generalized center-manifold reduction yields closed evolution equations for the macroscopic order parameters. More broadly, our work establishes a unified and versatile dynamical framework for analyzing collective phenomena in higher-dimensional Kuramoto-type models, opening the door to systematic studies of disorder, finite-size effects, and further extensions in higher-dimensional Kuramoto-like models.

III
Formulation of the model

We first recall the set-up of the usual two-dimensional Kuramoto model. The model in presence of annealed disorder and higher-harmonic interactions involves a system of
N
phase-only oscillators. The phase of the
j
-th oscillator at time
t
is denoted by the angle variable
\theta_{j}(t)\in[0,2\pi)
, with
j=1,2,\ldots,N
. Considering the interaction between the oscillators to be all-to-all, the time evolution is given by
84
:

\displaystyle\frac{d\theta_{j}}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{j}+\frac{K_{1}}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sin(\theta_{k}-\theta_{j})+\frac{K_{2}}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sin 2(\theta_{k}-\theta_{j})

(1)

\displaystyle+\sqrt{2T}~\eta_{j}(t),

where the annealed disorder
\eta_{j}(t)
is a Gaussian, white noise acting upon the oscillator
j
, with the properties
\langle\eta_{j}(t)\rangle=0
and
\langle\eta_{i}(t)\eta_{j}(t^{\prime})\rangle=\delta_{ij}\delta(t-t^{\prime})~\forall~i,j
. The notation
\langle\cdot\rangle
denotes averaging over noise realizations. The coefficient
K_{1}
is the strength of the fundamental coupling, i.e., the strength of the first-harmonic interaction, while
K_{2}
is the strength of the second-harmonic interaction, which is a representative higher-harmonic interaction. In Eq. (
1
), the natural frequencies
\omega_{j}\in(-\infty,\infty)
of the oscillators are quenched-disordered random variables distributed according to a given probability distribution
g(\omega)
, which has a finite mean
\omega_{0}\geq 0
and a finite width
\sigma\geq 0
. Examples of such distributions are uniform, Lorentzian, Gaussian, etc. The above model may also be interpreted as a model of continuous classical XY spins with mean-field interactions, driven out-of-equilibrium, and with
T
playing the role of the temperature (in suitable units) of a heat bath in contact
44
.

Putting
T=0
and
K_{2}=0
in Eq. (
1
) reduces the system to the original Kuramoto model
56
with only first-harmonic interaction. Reference
19
has recently generalized this limit of the model to general
D
dimensions, where
D=2
corresponds to the Kuramoto model. In line with this work, in this section, we will generalize the noisy Kuramoto model with first-harmonic and second-harmonic interaction, as described by Eq. (
1
), to a
D
-dimensional noisy Kuramoto model. Before considering the general
D
-dimensional case, let us first write Eq. (
1
) in a convenient vector form in two dimensions. In the context of Eq. (
1
), we may consider each oscillator as a point particle moving on a circle with unit radius. Let the position vector of the
j
-th oscillator from the centre of the circle be denoted by
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}
. The phase
\theta_{j}
thus becomes the angle that the vector
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}
forms with a given axis, say, the
x
-axis. The vector
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}
may then be written as
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}=\cos{\theta_{j}}~\hat{\textbf{x}}+\sin{\theta_{j}}~\hat{\textbf{y}}
, where
\hat{\mathrm{\textbf{x}}}
and
\hat{\mathrm{\textbf{y}}}
are the unit vector along the
x
- and the
y
-axis, respectively. Clearly, the magnitude of the vector is
|\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}|=1
. Taking derivative with respect to time, we obtain

\frac{d\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}}{dt}=\left(-\sin{\theta_{j}}~\hat{\textbf{x}}+\cos{\theta_{j}}~\hat{\textbf{y}}\right)\frac{d\theta_{j}}{dt}.

(2)

From the transformation relation between the Cartesian
(x,y)
and the polar
(r,\theta)
coordinates, we may express the unit vector along the radial direction as
\hat{\textbf{r}}=\cos{\theta}~\hat{\textbf{x}}+\sin{\theta}~\hat{\textbf{y}}
and the unit vector along the azimuthal direction as
\hat{\bm{\theta}}=-\sin{\theta}~\hat{\mathrm{\textbf{x}}}+\cos{\theta}~\hat{\mathrm{\textbf{y}}}
. On using Eq. (
1
), Eq. (
2
) may then be written as

\displaystyle\frac{d\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}}{dt}=\mathbf{W}_{j}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}+\frac{K_{1}}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sin(\theta_{k}-\theta_{j})~\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{j}

\displaystyle+\frac{2K_{2}}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\cos(\theta_{k}-\theta_{j})\sin(\theta_{k}-\theta_{j})~\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{j}+\sqrt{2T}\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j},

(3)

where
\textbf{W}_{j}
is the antisymmetric frequency matrix and
\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)
is the antisymmetric noise matrix, given respectively by

\mathbf{W}_{j}\equiv\begin{bmatrix}0&-\omega_{j}\\
\omega_{j}&0\end{bmatrix},~~\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)\equiv\begin{bmatrix}0&-\eta_{j}(t)\\
\eta_{j}(t)&0\end{bmatrix}.

(4)

Using the properties of
\eta_{j}(t)
, we obtain

\langle\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)\rangle=\mathbb{O}_{2},~~\langle\mathbf{N}_{i}(t)\mathbf{N}_{j}(t^{\prime})\rangle=-\delta_{ij}\delta(t-t^{\prime})\mathbf{I}_{2}~\forall~i,j,

(5)

where
\mathbb{O}_{2}
is a
2\times 2
matrix with all elements equal to zero, while
\mathbf{I}_{2}
is a
2\times 2
identity matrix. Using basic trigonometry, one can show that
19

\cos(\theta_{k}-\theta_{j})=\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}
and
\sin(\theta_{k}-\theta_{j})\,\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{j}=\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}-\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}\right)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}
. Equation (
3
) then yields

\displaystyle\frac{d\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}}{dt}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{W}_{j}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}+\frac{K_{1}}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}-\frac{K_{1}}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}\right)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}

\displaystyle+\frac{2K_{2}}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}\right)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}-\frac{2K_{2}}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}\right)^{2}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{2T}~\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}.

(6)

We may now define two order parameters, characterizing macroscopic order in the system, in the following manner:

\displaystyle\vec{\mathbb{P}}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k},

(7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}.

(8)

Using the above definition of the order parameters, we may rewrite Eq. (
6
) as

\displaystyle\frac{d\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}}{dt}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{W}_{j}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}+K_{1}\left[\vec{\mathbb{P}}-\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\cdot\vec{\mathbb{P}}\right)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\right]

\displaystyle+2K_{2}\Big[\mathbb{M}-\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\cdot\mathbb{M}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\right)\mathbf{I}_{2}\Big]\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}+\sqrt{2T}~\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}.

(9)

Note that Eq. (
9
) has the structure of a single dynamical variable
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}
evolving in presence of a mean “field”
\vec{\mathbb{P}}
and a mean “rotation”
\mathbb{M}
produced by all the dynamical variables.

Before proceeding, let us list down some relevant properties of the order parameters. Clearly, the norm of the vector
\vec{\mathbb{P}}
, given by
\mathbb{P}\equiv|\vec{\mathbb{P}}|=\sqrt{\vec{\mathbb{P}}\cdot\vec{\mathbb{P}}}
, satisfies
0\leq\mathbb{P}\leq 1
, as follows from the fact that
|\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}|=1~\forall~k
. In the fully-synchronized phase, when all the
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}
’s point along the same direction, we have
\mathbb{P}=1
. In the incoherent/unsynchronized phase, when all the
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}
’s are spread out over the entire circle, we have
\mathbb{P}=0
. Thus, the quantity
\mathbb{P}
characterizes qualitatively-different macroscopic behavior and is a measure of global macroscopic order in the system. For the two-dimensional case that is being discussed here, the quantity
\mathbb{P}
is exactly the Kuramoto order parameter
r_{1}

56
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}=r_{1}\equiv\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}e^{i\theta_{k}}\right|.

(10)

Note that the evolution equation (
9
) preserves the norm of each
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}
, as may be seen in the following way:

\displaystyle\frac{d\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\right)}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2~\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\cdot\frac{d\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}}{dt}

(11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2~\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{W}_{j}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}+2\sqrt{2T}~\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}.

The anti-symmetric structure of
\mathbf{W}_{j}
and
\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)
ensures that each term on the right hand side is zero, and hence,
d\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\right)/dt=0
, implying
|\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}|=1~\forall~j
at all times.

Let us turn to the order parameter
\mathbb{M}
. The unit norm of each
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}
immediately gives
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbb{M}\right]=1
. The definition (
8
) further implies that
\mathbb{M}
is a symmetric matrix, i.e.,
\mathbb{M}^{\top}=\mathbb{M}
, where
\top
denotes transpose operation. Defining the second Kuramoto-Daido
56
order parameter
z_{2}\equiv r_{2}e^{i2\psi_{2}}\equiv N^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{N}e^{i2\theta_{k}}
, we may express
\mathbb{M}
for the two-dimensional case as

\mathbb{M}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}1+\Re\left(z_{2}\right)&\Im\left(z_{2}\right)\\
\Im\left(z_{2}\right)&1-\Re\left(z_{2}\right)\end{bmatrix},

(12)

where
\Re\left(\cdot\right)
and
\Im\left(\cdot\right)
denote the real and the imaginary part of the quantity inside the brackets, respectively. In Section
III.1
, we discuss the physical property that is captured by the order parameter
\mathbb{M}
.

In the above backdrop, it is now easy to generalize Eqs. (
6
) and (
9
) to the
D
-dimensional case, when each of the
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}
’s may be considered a
D
-dimensional vector, with time evolution given by Eq. (
6
) or (
9
) with
\mathbf{I}_{2}
replaced by
\mathbf{I}_{D}
, where
\mathbf{I}_{D}
denotes
D\times D
identity matrix. The vectors
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}
now have their ends on the surface of a hyper-sphere of unit radius that is embedded in a
D
-dimensional space. The frequency matrix
\mathbf{W}_{j}
is now a
D\times D
antisymmetric matrix, known as the natural rotation
19
, and given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{j}\equiv\begin{bmatrix}0&\omega^{(j)}_{12}&\omega^{(j)}_{13}&\cdots&\omega^{(j)}_{1D}\\
-\omega^{(j)}_{12}&0&\omega^{(j)}_{23}&\cdots&\omega^{(j)}_{2D}\\
-\omega^{(j)}_{13}&-\omega^{(j)}_{23}&0&\cdots&\omega^{(j)}_{3D}\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\ddots&\vdots\\
-\omega^{(j)}_{1D}&-\omega^{(j)}_{2D}&-\omega^{(j)}_{3D}&\cdots&0\end{bmatrix}

(13)

in the Cartesian coordinate system, where each of these
\omega^{(j)}_{lm}
is sampled independently from a distribution
g(\omega)
. Clearly, there are
D(D-1)/2
independent elements in each natural rotation matrix
\mathbf{W}_{j}
. Alternatively, we may consider each matrix
\mathbf{W}_{j}
to be sampled from the ensemble of random antisymmetric matrices following the distribution
G(\mathbf{W})
. For the
D
-dimensional case, the noise matrix becomes

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)\equiv\begin{bmatrix}0&\eta^{(j)}_{12}(t)&\eta^{(j)}_{13}(t)&\cdots&\eta^{(j)}_{1D}(t)\\
-\eta^{(j)}_{12}(t)&0&\eta^{(j)}_{23}(t)&\cdots&\eta^{(j)}_{2D}(t)\\
-\eta^{(j)}_{13}(t)&-\eta^{(j)}_{23}(t)&0&\cdots&\eta^{(j)}_{3D}(t)\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\ddots&\vdots\\
-\eta^{(j)}_{1D}(t)&-\eta^{(j)}_{2D}(t)&-\eta^{(j)}_{3D}(t)&\cdots&0\end{bmatrix}~~~~~

(14)

in the Cartesian coordinate system, with the properties
\langle\eta^{(j)}_{lm}(t)\rangle=0
and
\langle\eta^{(i)}_{lm}(t)\eta^{(j)}_{l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})\rangle=\delta_{ij}\delta_{ll^{\prime}}\delta_{mm^{\prime}}\delta(t-t^{\prime})
, with
m>l
and
m^{\prime}>l^{\prime}
. Using these properties, we further get

\langle\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)\rangle=\mathbb{O}_{D},~~\langle\mathbf{N}_{i}(t)\mathbf{N}_{j}(t^{\prime})\rangle=-(D-1)\delta_{ij}\delta(t-t^{\prime})\mathbf{I}_{D},

(15)

for all
i,j
, with
\mathbb{O}_{D}
being a
D\times D
matrix with all entries equal to zero. Lastly, the order parameter
\vec{\mathbb{P}}
is a
D\times 1
column vector, while the order parameter
\mathbb{M}
is a
D\times D
symmetric matrix. Hence, the total number of independent quantities in the two order parameters is
D(D+3)/2-1
.

In this paper, we are going to consider the case without any quenched disorder, i.e.,
\mathbf{W}_{j}=\mathbf{W}~\forall~j
, since our main objective to investigate the interplay of annealed disorder and higher-harmonic interactions, as outlined in the Introduction. The ensuing calculations even in this identical natural-rotation case are quite involved, warranting a separate study of the case with quenched disorder
92
. For the case without any quenched disorder, using a trasformation
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\to e^{-\mathbf{W}t}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}~\forall~j
, Eq. (
6
) can be reduced to

\displaystyle\frac{d\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}}{dt}

\displaystyle=\frac{K_{1}}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}-\frac{K_{1}}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}\right)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}

\displaystyle+\frac{2K_{2}}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}\right)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}-\frac{2K_{2}}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}\right)^{2}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{2T}~\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}.

(16)

From now on, we will consider that such a transformation has been implemented, and hence, will study the dynamics (
16
).

III.1
Discussion on
\mathbb{M}

Here we discuss the properties of the order parameter
\mathbb{M}
. For simplicity, let us first focus on three dimensions. If we denote the position vector of the
j
-th oscillator in the Cartesian coordinate as
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}=(\sigma^{\mathrm{x}}_{j},\sigma^{\mathrm{y}}_{j},\sigma^{\mathrm{z}}_{j})
, then from the definition (
8
), we obtain

\mathbb{M}=\begin{bmatrix}\overline{(\sigma^{\mathrm{x}})^{2}}&\overline{\sigma^{\mathrm{x}}\sigma^{\mathrm{y}}}&\overline{\sigma^{\mathrm{x}}\sigma^{\mathrm{z}}}\\
\overline{\sigma^{\mathrm{y}}\sigma^{\mathrm{x}}}&\overline{(\sigma^{\mathrm{y}})^{2}}&\overline{\sigma^{\mathrm{y}}\sigma^{\mathrm{z}}}\\
\overline{\sigma^{\mathrm{z}}\sigma^{\mathrm{x}}}&\overline{\sigma^{\mathrm{z}}\sigma^{\mathrm{y}}}&\overline{(\sigma^{\mathrm{z}})^{2}}\end{bmatrix},

(17)

where we have defined

\overline{\sigma^{\mathrm{a}}\sigma^{\mathrm{b}}}\equiv\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sigma^{\mathrm{a}}_{j}\sigma^{\mathrm{b}}_{j},

(18)

with
\mathrm{a,b}=\mathrm{x,y,z}
. Hence, the quantity
\overline{\sigma^{\mathrm{a}}\sigma^{\mathrm{b}}}
denotes the correlation between the
\mathrm{a}
-th and the
\mathrm{b}
-th Cartesian component of the position vector of the oscillators. Clearly, this is valid in any general dimension
D
, where the matrix element
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{ab}}
denotes the correlation between the
\mathrm{a}
-th and the
\mathrm{b}
-th Cartesian component of the position vector of the oscillators, and
\mathbb{M}
denotes a correlation matrix.

In the particular incoherent state where all the oscillators are uniformly spread out on the surface of the
D
-dimensional hyper-sphere (the uniformly incoherent state), one has
\mathbb{P}=0
. Moreover, there is no correlation between the different components of the position vector. Hence, the off-diagonal terms of the matrix
\mathbb{M}
will all be zero. Further, since in this state, there is no preferred coordinate axis, the diagonal terms of the matrix
\mathbb{M}
, which denote self-correlation of different Cartesian components, should be all equal. The sum of these diagonal elements gives the trace of the matrix
\mathbb{M}
, which we have already seen to be equal to unity. Hence, all the diagonal terms of the matrix
\mathbb{M}
have the value
1/D
in the uniformly incoherent state, yielding
\mathbb{M}=D^{-1}\mathbf{I}_{D}
. In a synchronized state with
\mathbb{P}\neq 0
, when the oscillators form one macroscopic cluster, there will be correlations between the different components of the position vector, making the off-diagonal terms of the matrix
\mathbb{M}
nonzero. Hence, in this state,
\mathbb{M}
deviates from
D^{-1}\mathbf{I}_{D}
.

Surprisingly, there can be states in which the oscillators form multiple clusters without generating any global order, the simplest scenario being two clusters of equal size at diametrically opposite position on the surface of the
D
-dimensional hyper sphere, as shown in Figure
1
(e). In these states, clearly, we have
\mathbb{P}=0
. Hence, such states cannot be distinguished from the incoherent state by the usual synchronization order parameter
\vec{\mathbb{P}}
. Consequently, any phase transition between such states and the incoherent state will be invisible to
\vec{\mathbb{P}}
. However, formation of clusters gives rise to correlations between the different components of the position vector, making
\mathbb{M}
deviate from
D^{-1}\mathbf{I}_{D}
. To identify these states with multiple clusters without global order as well as to probe the associated transition, we need the correlation-based order parameter
\mathbb{M}
.

III.2
Symmetry of the model

Before moving ahead, let us first understand the symmetry of the model given in Eq. (
16
). Since rotation matrices
\mathbf{R}
are orthogonal transformations satisfying
\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}^{\top}=\mathbf{R}^{\top}\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{I}_{D}
, their application on any vector preserves its Euclidean norm. Consequently, acting with any rotation matrix
\mathbf{R}
on any unit vector
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}
does not change its length, and the rotated vector
\mathbf{R}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}
remains on the surface of the
D
-dimensional unit sphere.

We now focus on obtaining the evolution equation of
\mathbf{R}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}
. Let all vectors
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}
be transformed by a fixed rotation matrix
\mathbf{R}
. Applying
\mathbf{R}
on both sides of Eq. (
16
) from the left along with the properties of the rotation matrices, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d\big(\mathbf{R}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\big)}{dt}=\frac{K_{1}}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathbf{R}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}-\frac{K_{1}}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left(\mathbf{R}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{R}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}\right)\mathbf{R}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}

\displaystyle+\frac{2K_{2}}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left(\mathbf{R}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{R}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}\right)\mathbf{R}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}-\frac{2K_{2}}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left(\mathbf{R}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{R}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}\right)^{2}\mathbf{R}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{k}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{2T}~\Big[\mathbf{R}\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)\mathbf{R}^{\top}\Big]\mathbf{R}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}.

(19)

We may define
\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{j}(t)\equiv\mathbf{R}\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)\mathbf{R}^{\top}
as the new noise matrix acting on the
j
-th oscillator. In Appendix
A
, we show that the properties of the rotated noise matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{j}(t)
are identical to those of
\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)
, which reads as

\langle\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{j}(t)\rangle=\mathbb{O}_{D},~~\langle\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{i}(t)\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{j}(t^{\prime})\rangle=-(D-1)\delta_{ij}\delta(t-t^{\prime})\mathbf{I}_{D}.

(20)

Thus, we may replace the noise terms
\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{j}(t)\equiv\mathbf{R}\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)\mathbf{R}^{\top}
in Eq. (
19
) with
\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)
itself. Further redefining the rotated vectors as
\vec{\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}}_{j}\equiv\mathbf{R}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}~\forall~j
, we may rewrite Eq. (
19
) as

\displaystyle\frac{d\vec{\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}}_{j}}{dt}

\displaystyle=\frac{K_{1}}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\vec{\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}}_{k}-\frac{K_{1}}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left(\vec{\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}}_{j}\cdot\vec{\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}}_{k}\right)\vec{\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}}_{j}

\displaystyle+\frac{2K_{2}}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left(\vec{\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}}_{j}\cdot\vec{\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}}_{k}\right)\vec{\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}}_{k}-\frac{2K_{2}}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left(\vec{\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}}_{j}\cdot\vec{\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}}_{k}\right)^{2}\vec{\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}}_{k}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{2T}~\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)\vec{\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}}_{j},

(21)

which is identical to Eq. (
16
). Hence, the evolution equations of the rotated vectors
\vec{\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}}_{j}\equiv\mathbf{R}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}~\forall~j
are identical to that of
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}_{j}~\forall~j
. Thus, we conclude that the action of any constant rotation matrix on all the vectors denoting the position of the oscillators keeps the evolution equation invariant, making it a symmetry of the problem. This knowledge about symmetry will be useful in doing the center manifold analysis in Sec.
V
.

IV
Fokker-Planck Equation

Our aim in this section is to study the system (
16
) in the thermodynamic limit
N\to\infty
. To this end, we may introduce a density function
F\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)
, denoting the density of oscillators at “position”
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
at time
t
. The normalization condition reads as

\displaystyle\int_{|\vec{\bm{\sigma}}|=1}F\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)d\vec{\bm{\sigma}}=1,

(22)

where the integration is done over the
(D-1)
-dimensional surface of the
D
-dimensional unit sphere, represented as
\mathscr{S}\equiv S^{D-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{D}
. We may now define a
D
-dimensional spherical polar coordinate system (see Appendix
B
), with
r\in[0,\infty),~\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\ldots,\theta_{D-2}\in[0,\pi]
and
\phi\in[0,2\pi)
. Then, a differential surface element on the unit sphere
\mathscr{S}
may be expressed as
d\Omega=d\phi\prod_{j=1}^{D-2}\sin^{D-1-j}{\theta_{j}}d\theta_{j}
, with the total area of the
(D-1)
-dimensional surface given by

\displaystyle\Omega_{D}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\Omega=\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\phi\prod_{j=1}^{D-2}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta_{j}\sin^{D-1-j}{\theta_{j}}d\theta_{j}

(23)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi^{D/2}}{\Gamma(D/2)},

and the differential volume element in
\mathbb{R}^{D}
given by
dV_{D}=r^{D-1}drd\Omega
. In their terms, Eq. (
22
) may be expressed as

\displaystyle\int F\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)~d\Omega=1.

(24)

To obtain the corresponding Fokker-Planck equation, following
19
, we first extend the velocity flow field given by Eq. (
16
) to the entire space
\mathbb{R}^{D}
, by defining
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}=\vec{\mathbf{r}}/r=\hat{\mathbf{r}}
. Using this, we may write the velocity flow field from Eq. (
16
) as

\frac{d\vec{\mathbf{r}}}{dt}=K_{1}\left[\vec{\mathbb{P}}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathbb{P}}\right)\vec{\mathbf{r}}\right]+2K_{2}\Bigg[\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{M}-\frac{1}{r^{3}}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\mathbb{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\right)\mathbf{I}_{D}\Bigg]\vec{\mathbf{r}}.

(25)

Since
\hat{\mathbf{r}}\cdot d\vec{\mathbf{r}}/dt=0
, these flow fields result in surfaces of spheres with varying radius
r
and centered at

r=0
as invariant manifolds under the dynamical evolution.

We may then extend the density function
F\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)
, which is defined on the surface
\mathscr{S}
, to the entire
\mathbb{R}^{D}
space, by defining the density
\mathscr{F}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}},t\right)
as

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}},t\right)=F\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)\delta(r-1).

(26)

Hence, the normalization condition (
24
) may be extended as

\displaystyle\int\mathscr{F}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}},t\right)~dV_{D}=1.

(27)

To obtain the time evolution of
\mathscr{F}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}},t\right)
, we start by considering a continuous and differentiable test function
h_{1}(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}(t))
defined on the surface
\mathscr{S}
and using the standard It
\hat{\mathrm{o}}
-calculus
33
, we obtain (see Appendix
C
)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathscr{F}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\vec{\nabla}\cdot\left[\mathscr{F}\vec{\mathbf{v}}\right]+\frac{T}{r^{2}}\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}\mathscr{F},

(28)

where the velocity field
\vec{\mathbf{v}}=d\vec{\mathbf{r}}/dt
is given in Eq. (
25
). Using the expression of
\vec{\nabla}
from Eq. (
175
) and integrating Eq. (
28
) over
r
from
1-\epsilon
to
1+\epsilon
for small
\epsilon
simplifies Eq. (
28
) into the following form (see Appendix
G
):

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F}{\partial t}=

\displaystyle-K_{1}\left[\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}F-(D-1)F\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right]\cdot\vec{\mathbb{P}}-2K_{2}F

\displaystyle-2K_{2}\left[\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}F-DF\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right]\cdot\mathbb{M}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}+T~\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}F.

(29)

The order parameters defined in Eqs. (
7
) and (
8
) take the form

\displaystyle\vec{\mathbb{P}}

\displaystyle=\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}~F\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)~d\Omega,

(30)

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}

\displaystyle=\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}~F\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)~d\Omega.

(31)

Since
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}F=0
, we may rearrange Eq. (
29
) into the following compact form:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F}{\partial t}=

\displaystyle-K_{1}\left[\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}F-(D-1)F\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right]\cdot\vec{\mathbb{P}}

\displaystyle-2K_{2}\left[\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}F-DF\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right]\cdot\left[\mathbb{M}-\frac{1}{D}\mathbf{I}\right]\vec{\bm{\sigma}}+T~\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}F.

(32)

V
Center Manifold Analysis

The Fokker-Planck equation (
32
) along with Eqs. (
30
) and (
31
) provides a closed-form description of the time evolution of the system in the thermodynamic limit. Consider the incoherent stationary state, when all the oscillators are uniformly spread out on the surface of the
D
-dimensional unit sphere. We may express the corresponding stationary-state density in the limit
N\to\infty
as

\displaystyle F_{0}\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)=\frac{1}{\Omega_{D}},~\mathrm{with}~\Omega_{D}=\frac{2\pi^{\frac{D}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{D}{2}\right)}.

(33)

Using this in Eqs. (
30
) and (
31
), we obtain

\displaystyle\vec{\mathbb{P}}_{F=F_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{S_{D}}\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}~d\Omega=\vec{0}_{D},

(34)

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{F=F_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{S_{D}}\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}~d\Omega=\frac{1}{D}\mathbf{I}_{D}.

(35)

where
\vec{0}_{D}
is a
D\times 1
vector with all elements zero. Using Eq. (
33
) along with Eqs. (
34
) and (
35
) in Eq. (
32
), we obtain that the uniform distribution
F_{0}
satisfies the Fokker-Planck equation in the stationary state.

Note that the incoherent state, characterised by
\vec{\mathbb{P}}=\vec{0}_{D}
, is satisfied by many possible incoherent state densities. Among all these incoherent state densities, we are interested in the stability of the uniformly-incoherent state given by Eq. (
33
). To study this, we initiate the dynamics in a state with small perturbation around the state
F_{0}\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)
and study the evolution of this perturbation under the dynamics (
32
). For fixed
K_{1}
,
K_{2}
and
D
, if the perturbation decreases to zero with time, then the uniformly-incoherent state
F_{0}\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)
is a stable solution of Eq. (
32
) and the system is in the incoherent phase. On the other hand, if the perturbation keeps on growing in time, then
F_{0}\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)
is an unstable solution of Eq. (
32
) and the system is in the synchronized phase.

In order to study the behavior of the aforementioned perturbation in time, we start by considering the probability density
F\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)
to be of the form

F\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)=\frac{1}{\Omega_{D}}+\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right),

(36)

where
|\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)|\ll 1
denotes the perturbation around the state
F_{0}\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)=1/\Omega_{D}
. Normalization of
F\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)
given in Eq. (
24
) puts the following condition:

\displaystyle\int\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)d\Omega=0.

(37)

Using Eq. (
36
) in Eq. (
32
), we obtain the evolution equation of
\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)
, which reads as

\frac{\partial\eta}{\partial t}=\mathscr{L}\eta+\mathscr{N}[\eta],

(38)

where the linear operator
\mathscr{L}
has the expression

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}\eta

\displaystyle=T~\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}\eta+K_{1}\bigg(\frac{D-1}{\Omega_{D}}\bigg)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime},t\right)~d\Omega^{\prime}

\displaystyle+2K_{2}\bigg(\frac{D}{\Omega_{D}}\bigg)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\bigg[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime},t\right)~d\Omega^{\prime}\bigg]\vec{\bm{\sigma}},

(39)

and the non-linear operator
\mathscr{N}[\cdot]
has the expression

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}[\eta]

\displaystyle=(D-1)K_{1}\eta\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime},t\right)~d\Omega^{\prime}

\displaystyle-K_{1}\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}\eta\cdot\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime},t\right)~d\Omega^{\prime}

\displaystyle+2DK_{2}\eta\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\bigg[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime},t\right)~d\Omega^{\prime}\bigg]\vec{\bm{\sigma}}

\displaystyle-2K_{2}\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}\eta\cdot\bigg[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime},t\right)~d\Omega^{\prime}\bigg]\vec{\bm{\sigma}}.

(40)

Since
\eta
is a small perturbation,
\mathscr{L}\eta
dominates over
\mathscr{N}[\eta]
. Hence, in the leading order, the dynamics of
\eta
is determined by the eigenspectrum of the operator
\mathscr{L}
. In passing, we define a new order parameter

\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}\equiv\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}~\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)~d\Omega=\mathbb{M}-\frac{1}{D}\mathbf{I}_{D},

(41)

where
\mathbb{M}
is defined in Eq. (
31
). From now on, we will use
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}
instead of
\mathbb{M}
to characterize the corresponding transition.

To build intuition before solving the problem for general
D
dimensions, let us first explicitly work out the case for
D=3
. We will then apply the insights so obtained in solving the problem for arbitrary dimensions.

V.1
D=3
Case

For illustrative purposes, let us assume
K_{2}=0
in this section. In
D=3
, the probability density
\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)
is a function of
\theta_{1},\phi
and
t
. Hence, quite generally, we may express it as

\eta(\theta_{1},\phi,t)=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-l}^{l}a_{lm}(t)~Y_{l}^{m}(\theta_{1},\phi),

(42)

where
Y_{l}^{m}(\theta_{1},\phi)
’s are the standard spherical harmonic functions. Here, the summation over
l
starts from
l=1
instead of
l=0
to satisfy Eq. (
37
).

By definition, spherical harmonics are eigenfunctions of
\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}
with the eigenvalue equation

\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}Y_{l}^{m}(\theta_{1},\phi)=-l(l+1)Y_{l}^{m}(\theta_{1},\phi).

(43)

From now on, for brevity of notation, we will use
Y_{l}^{m}
to denote
Y_{l}^{m}(\theta_{1},\phi)
. In Cartesian coordinates, any arbitrary unit vector may be written as (see Appendix
B
)

\displaystyle\vec{\bm{\sigma}}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos{\theta_{1}}\\
\sin{\theta_{1}}\cos{\phi}\\
\sin{\theta_{1}}\sin{\phi}\end{pmatrix}=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{3}}\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{2}Y_{0}^{0}\\
Y_{1}^{-1}-Y_{1}^{1}\\
iY_{1}^{-1}+iY_{1}^{1}\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(44)

Note that the convention of naming the Cartesian axes is as given in Appendix
B
, which differs from the usual convention.

Using Eq. (
44
) along with the orthonormality properties of spherical harmonics, we may write

\vec{\mathbb{P}}=\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime},t\right)~d\Omega^{\prime}=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{3}}\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{2}a_{1,0}\\
a_{1,-1}-a_{11}\\
-ia_{1,1}-ia_{1,-1}\end{pmatrix},

(45)

using which we obtain

\frac{K_{1}}{2\pi}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime},t\right)~d\Omega^{\prime}=\frac{2K_{1}}{3}\sum_{m=-1}^{1}a_{1m}Y_{1}^{m}.

(46)

Combining Eq. (
46
) along with Eq. (
43
), we obtain that
Y_{l}^{m}
is also an eigenfunction of the linear operator
\mathscr{L}
(with
K_{2}=0
) with the eigenvalue equation

\mathscr{L}Y_{l}^{m}=\lambda_{l}Y_{l}^{m},

(47)

and with the eigenvalue

\lambda_{l}=-Tl(l+1)+\frac{2K_{1}}{3}\delta_{l,1}.

(48)

Note that since the eigenvalues
\lambda_{l}
are independent of
m
, each eigenvalue has a degeneracy
(2l+1)
. This is because for a fixed
l
, there are
(2l+1)
number of
Y_{l}^{m}
’s possible.

On the basis of the above, we here conclude that in Eq. (
42
),
\eta
is expanded in the eigenbasis of the operator
\mathscr{L}
. Furthermore, since for a fixed
l
, the functions
Y_{l}^{m}
for all
m
have identical eigenvalue
\lambda_{l}
, any general linear combination
\sum_{m=-l}^{l}c_{m}Y_{l}^{m}
will also be an eigenfunction of
\mathscr{L}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{l}
. Hence, for a fixed
l
, instead of using
Y_{l}^{m}
as our expansion basis for
\eta
, we may use a different basis that is simultaneously an eigenbasis of
\mathscr{L}
and also suitable for invoking the symmetry of the problem as discussed in Sec.
III.2
.

Another important observation is that any
Y_{l}^{m}
with
l>1
may be expressed as a linear combination of the product of
Y_{1}^{-1},~Y_{1}^{0}
, and
Y_{1}^{1}
. For example, we have

\displaystyle Y_{2}^{-2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{10\pi}{3}}Y_{1}^{-1}Y_{1}^{-1},~~Y_{2}^{-1}=\sqrt{\frac{20\pi}{3}}Y_{1}^{-1}Y_{1}^{0},

\displaystyle Y_{2}^{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{20\pi}{9}}\bigg[Y_{1}^{-1}Y_{1}^{1}+Y_{1}^{0}Y_{1}^{0}\bigg],

\displaystyle Y_{2}^{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{20\pi}{3}}Y_{1}^{1}Y_{1}^{0},~~Y_{2}^{2}=\sqrt{\frac{10\pi}{3}}Y_{1}^{1}Y_{1}^{1}.

Note that this decomposition is similar to addition of angular momentum, whereby eigenstates of total angular momentum are expressed in a product basis. Hence, the coefficents in the expansion in Eq. (
) are simply the well-known Clebsch–Gordan coefficients. More generally, any
Y_{l}^{m}
for general
(l,m)
may be written as
94

\displaystyle Y_{l}^{m}(\theta_{1},\phi)=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{(4\pi)^{n-1}(2n+1)!!}{3^{n}n!}}

\displaystyle\times\{\ldots\{\{\vec{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}\otimes\vec{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}\}_{2}\otimes\vec{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}\}_{3}\ldots\otimes\vec{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}\}_{lm},

(50)

where we have
\vec{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}=\big(Y_{1}^{-1}(\theta_{1},\phi),Y_{1}^{0}(\theta_{1},\phi),Y_{1}^{1}(\theta_{1},\phi)\big)
. Here
\{A_{\ell_{1}}\otimes B_{\ell_{2}}\}_{\ell}
denotes the irreducible spherical tensor of rank
\ell
obtained by coupling two spherical tensors of ranks
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
.
The projection onto angular momentum
\ell
is carried out via the standard Clebsch-Gordan decomposition of the tensor product.

With this insight, let us now focus on the expansion in Eq. (
42
). Using Eq. (
), clearly, we may write

\displaystyle\sum_{m=-1}^{1}a_{1m}(t)~Y_{1}^{m}(\theta_{1},\phi)

\displaystyle=\vec{\mathbf{a}}\cdot\vec{\mathbb{Y}}_{1},

(51)

\displaystyle\sum_{m=-2}^{2}a_{2m}(t)~Y_{2}^{m}(\theta_{1},\phi)

\displaystyle=\vec{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{b}\vec{\mathbb{Y}}_{1},

(52)

where
\vec{\mathbf{a}}=\big(a_{1,-1}(t),a_{1,0}(t),a_{1,1}(t)\big)
and

\mathbf{b}=\sqrt{\frac{5\pi}{9}}\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{6}a_{2,-2}(t)&\sqrt{3}a_{2,-1}(t)&a_{2,0}(t)\\
\sqrt{3}a_{2,-1}(t)&\sqrt{4}a_{2,0}(t)&\sqrt{3}a_{2,1}(t)\\
a_{2,0}(t)&\sqrt{3}a_{2,1}(t)&\sqrt{6}a_{2,2}(t)\end{pmatrix}.

(53)

Hence, we may rewrite Eq. (
42
) as

\eta(\theta_{1},\phi,t)=\vec{\mathbf{a}}\cdot\vec{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}+\vec{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{b}\vec{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}+\ldots.

(54)

We now express
\vec{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}
in terms of
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
. From Eq. (
44
), we have

\vec{\bm{\sigma}}=\Xi^{-1}\vec{\mathbb{Y}}_{1},~~~\Xi^{-1}=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{3}}\begin{pmatrix}0&\sqrt{2}&0\\
1&0&-1\\
i&0&i\end{pmatrix},

(55)

inverting which, we may write

\vec{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}=\Xi~\vec{\bm{\sigma}},~~~\Xi=\sqrt{\frac{3}{8\pi}}\begin{pmatrix}0&1&-i\\
\sqrt{2}&0&0\\
0&-1&-i\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(56)

Using Eq. (
56
) in Eq. (
54
), we may express
\eta
as

\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)=\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}+\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}+\ldots,

(57)

where we have
\vec{\mathbf{A}}=\Xi^{\top}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
and
\mathbf{B}=\Xi^{\top}\mathbf{b}\Xi
. Direct computation gives us
\mathbf{B}^{\top}=\mathbf{B}
and
\mathrm{Tr}[\mathbf{B}]=0
. Representing
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}),~\vec{\mathbf{A}}=(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})
and
ij
-th element of
\mathbf{B}
as
B_{ij}
in the Cartesian coordinates, we may rewrite Eq. (
57
) as

\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)=\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{3}A_{i_{1}}\sigma_{i_{1}}+\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{3}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{3}B_{i_{1}i_{2}}\sigma_{i_{1}}\sigma_{i_{2}}+\ldots,

(58)

with
\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{3}B_{i_{1}i_{1}}=0
. More generally, combining Eq. (
50
) along with Eq. (
56
), we may write the general expansion of
\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)
in the following way: If
\mathbb{T}^{(n)}
is a symmetric traceless tensor of order
n
with
T^{(n)}_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n}}
its
i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{n}
-th element, then we may express
\eta
as
59
;
6

\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{3}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{3}\cdots\sum_{i_{n}=1}^{3}T^{(n)}_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n}}\sigma_{i_{1}}\sigma_{i_{2}}\ldots\sigma_{i_{n}},

(59)

where
\mathbb{T}^{(n)}
being symmetric implies that swapping of any indices does not change it, i.e.,

T^{(n)}_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n}}=T^{(n)}_{i_{P(1)},i_{P(2)},\ldots,i_{P(n)}},

(60)

with
P
being any arbitrary permutation of the indices, and traceless means contracting any two indices gives zero, i.e.,

\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{3}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{3}\cdots\sum_{i_{n}=1}^{3}\delta_{i_{p}i_{q}}T^{(n)}_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{p},\ldots,i_{q},\ldots,i_{n}}=0,

(61)

for arbitrary
p,q
. Comparing Eq. (
59
) with Eq. (
58
) we observe that
\mathbb{T}^{(1)}\equiv\vec{\mathbf{A}}
and
\mathbb{T}^{(2)}\equiv\mathbf{B}
. We may introduce a compact notation

\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big),

(62)

where we have

\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big)\equiv\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{3}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{3}\cdots\sum_{i_{n}=1}^{3}T^{(n)}_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n}}\sigma_{i_{1}}\sigma_{i_{2}}\ldots\sigma_{i_{n}}.

(63)

V.2
General
D
-Dimensional Case

Having now an explicit understanding of the structure of
\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)
in
D=3
, here we generalize our discussions to any general dimension
D
; here, we will consider the general case with
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
both non-zero. Note that Eq. (
57
) as well as Eq. (
62
) can be generalized to
D
dimensions with the extension that both
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{D})
and
\vec{\mathbf{A}}=(A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{D})
are
D
-dimensional vectors,
\mathbf{B}
is a
D\times D
matrix,

\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big)=\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{D}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{D}\cdots\sum_{i_{n}=1}^{D}T^{(n)}_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n}}\sigma_{i_{1}}\sigma_{i_{2}}\ldots\sigma_{i_{n}},

(64)

and the traceless property reads as

\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{D}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{D}\cdots\sum_{i_{n}=1}^{D}\delta_{i_{p}i_{q}}T^{(n)}_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{p},\ldots,i_{q},\ldots,i_{n}}=0.

(65)

Before moving on, we have to first prove that Eq. (
62
) is still an useful expansion in general
D
dimensions. In Appendix
I
, we prove that
\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big)
is an eigenfunction of
\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}
with the eigenvalue equation

\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big)=-n(n+D-2)\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big).

(66)

Moreover, in Appendix
J
, we derive that

\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime\otimes n}\Big)d\Omega^{\prime}=\delta_{n,1}\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big),

(67)

and in Appendix
M
, we derive that

\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\bigg[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime\otimes n}\Big)d\Omega^{\prime}\bigg]\vec{\bm{\sigma}}=\frac{\delta_{n,2}2\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big).

(68)

Putting Eqs. (
66
), (
67
), and (
68
) back into Eq. (
39
), we observe that
\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big)
is an eigenfunction of
\mathscr{L}
with the eigenvalue equation

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big)=\lambda_{n}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big),

(69)

where the corresponding eigenvalues are

\lambda_{n}=-n(n+D-2)T+\frac{(D-1)K_{1}}{D}\delta_{n,1}+\frac{4K_{2}}{(D+2)}\delta_{n,2}.

(70)

Note that putting
D=3
and
K_{2}=0
in Eq. (
69
), we get back Eq. (
48
).

In a symmetric traceless tensor
\mathbb{T}^{(n)}
of order
n
, the total number of independent elements is given by

\mathrm{dim}\Big[\mathbb{T}^{(n)}\Big]=\begin{pmatrix}D+n-1\\
n\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}D+n-3\\
n-2\end{pmatrix}.

(71)

Hence, each of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}
has degeneracy
\mathrm{dim}\big[\mathbb{T}^{(n)}\big]
. Clearly, eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}
has degeneracy
D
, eigenvalue
\lambda_{2}
has degeneracy
D(D+1)/2-1
, and so on.

In the light of the above discussion, we conclude that the expansion of
\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)
in Eq. (
62
) is done in the eigenbasis of the operator
\mathscr{L}
.

V.3
Effect of Symmetry

Let us consider arbitrary
D
dimensions and go back to the expansion in Eq. (
62
), which provides the general form of
\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)
. Writing the first few terms, we get

\displaystyle\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(1)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)+\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(2)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)+\cdots,

\displaystyle=\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}+\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}+\cdots,

(72)

where
\vec{\mathbf{A}}
and
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
are
D
-dimensional vectors and
\mathbf{B}
is a
D\times D
symmetric traceless matrix.

Consider all the vectors denoting the position of the oscillators being rotated by a constant rotation matrix
\mathbf{R}
. Hence, the position vector
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
is transformed to
\vec{\bm{\widetilde{\sigma}}}\equiv\mathbf{R}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
and the probability density
\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)
is transformed to
\widetilde{\eta}(\vec{\bm{\widetilde{\sigma}}},t)
. Since
Eq. (
72
) provides the general form of probability density on
\mathscr{S}
, the probability density
\widetilde{\eta}(\vec{\bm{\widetilde{\sigma}}},t)
can also be expressed as

\widetilde{\eta}(\vec{\bm{\widetilde{\sigma}}},t)=\vec{\mathbf{\widetilde{A}}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\widetilde{\sigma}}}+\vec{\bm{\widetilde{\sigma}}}\cdot\mathbf{\widetilde{B}}\vec{\bm{\widetilde{\sigma}}}+\cdots.

(73)

Using
\vec{\bm{\widetilde{\sigma}}}\equiv\mathbf{R}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
in Eq. (
73
), we obtain

\widetilde{\eta}(\vec{\bm{\widetilde{\sigma}}},t)=\Big(\mathbf{R}^{\top}\vec{\mathbf{\widetilde{A}}}\Big)\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}+\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\Big(\mathbf{R}^{\top}\mathbf{\widetilde{B}}\mathbf{R}\Big)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}+\cdots.

(74)

From the discussion in Sec.
III.2
, the system remains invariant under rotation by any constant
\mathbf{R}
. Probability conservation implies

\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)=\widetilde{\eta}(\vec{\bm{\widetilde{\sigma}}},t)~\mathrm{det}\big[\mathbf{R}\big],

(75)

where
\mathrm{det}\big[\mathbf{R}\big]
is the determinant of the Jacobian. From the properties of the rotation matrix, we have
\mathrm{det}\big[\mathbf{R}\big]=1
. Using this along with Eqs. (
72
) and (
74
) in Eq. (
75
) and comparing both sides, we obtain

\vec{\mathbf{\widetilde{A}}}=\mathbf{R}\vec{\mathbf{A}},~~~\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}^{\top}.

(76)

Hence, the rotational symmetry of the problem restricts
\vec{\mathbf{A}}
and
\mathbf{B}
to transform following Eq. (
76
). Going in this way, we may say, for any general traceless symmetric tensor
\mathbb{T}^{(n)}
, it has to transform following the rule

\widetilde{T}^{(n)}_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n}}=\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{D}\cdots\sum_{j_{n}=1}^{D}T^{(n)}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{n}}R_{i_{1}j_{1}}R_{i_{2}j_{2}}\cdots R_{i_{n}j_{n}},

(77)

where
R_{ab}
is
ab
-th component of
\mathbf{R}
and
\widetilde{T}^{(n)}_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n}}
are the components of the rotated tensor
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}^{(n)}
. Note that, the transformation rule (
77
) preserves the symmetric and traceless properties of the tensor
\mathbb{T}^{(n)}
.

V.3.1
Critical Point

We now focus on the eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}
, given in Eq. (
70
). For
n\geq 3
, the eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}=-n(n+D-2)T
are independent of the interaction strengths
K_{1},K_{2}
and always negative. Hence, any perturbation given by the eigenfunctions corresponding to these eigenvalues or linear combinations thereof will quickly die down in time and go to zero under evolution solely due to the linear operator in Eq. (
38
). On the other hand, considering the eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}
, upon changing
K_{1}
, it changes sign at
K_{1}=K_{1}^{\mathrm{c}}\equiv DT
. Similarly, upon changing
K_{2}
, eigenvalue
\lambda_{2}
changes sign at
K_{2}=K_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}\equiv D(D+2)T/2
. When
K_{1}<K_{1}^{\mathrm{c}}\equiv DT
and
K_{2}<K_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}\equiv D(D+2)T/2
, the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
are negative. Since the rest of the eigenvalues are also negative, any perturbation will go to zero in time in this regime, making the uniformly incoherent state stable in the region
K_{1}<K_{1}^{\mathrm{c}}
and
K_{2}<K_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
.

For
K_{2}\leq K_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
and
K_{1}>K_{1}^{\mathrm{c}}
, we have
\lambda_{1}>0
and rest of the eigenvalues negative. Hence, any perturbation of the form
\big(\mathbb{T}^{(1)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)\equiv\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
, which is an eigenfunction of
\mathscr{L}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}
, will become linearly unstable. As a result, when
\lambda_{1}>0
, any perturbation in its eigenspace grows with time. Hence, in the leading order,
\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)
will be of the form
\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
. From the results in Appendix
L
, we have
\vec{\mathbb{P}}=\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)~d\Omega\approx\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)d\Omega=\big(\Omega_{D}/D\big)\vec{\mathbf{A}}
. Since the perturbations having the form
\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
grows in time, the quantity
\vec{\mathbf{A}}
also grows in time, indicating
\vec{\mathbb{P}}
becomes nonzero in this range. Hence, the order parameter
\vec{\mathbb{P}}
shows a phase transition at

K_{1}^{\mathrm{c}}=DT.

(78)

This shows that the critical point of
\vec{\mathbb{P}}
is independent of the interaction strength
K_{2}
as long as
K_{2}\leq K_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
. On the other hand, the nature of the criticality depends on
K_{2}
, which we will discuss in the next section. Note that when the perturbation grows sufficiently large,
\mathscr{N}[\eta]
starts contributing in the dynamics of
\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)
in the next leading order. From the discussion in Sec.
V.4
, this contribution is of the form
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
. Hence, up to the second leading order, we have
\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)\approx\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}+\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
. From the results in Appendix
L
, we further have
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}=\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)~d\Omega=\big[2\Omega_{D}/D(D+2)\big]\mathbf{B}
. This indicates that order parameter
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}
also shows a transition at
K_{1}=K_{1}^{\mathrm{c}}
.

For
K_{1}\leq K_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
and
K_{2}>K_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
, we have
\lambda_{2}>0
and rest of the eigenvalues negative. Hence, any perturbation of the form
\big(\mathbb{T}^{(2)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)\equiv\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
, which is an eigenfunction of
\mathscr{L}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{2}
, will become linearly unstable. When
\lambda_{2}>0
, any perturbation in its eigenspace grows with time. Hence, in the leading order,
\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)
will be of the form
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
. From the results in Appendix
L
, we have
\vec{\mathbb{P}}=\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)~d\Omega\approx\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)d\Omega=\vec{0}_{D}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}=\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)~d\Omega\approx\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)d\Omega=\big\{2\Omega_{D}/[D(D+2)]\}\mathbf{B}
. Since the perturbations having the form
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
grows in time, the quantity
\mathbf{B}
also grows in time, indicating
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}
becomes nonzero in this range. Hence, the order parameter
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}
shows a phase transition at

K_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}=\frac{D(D+2)T}{2},

(79)

while the order-parameter
\vec{\mathbb{P}}
does not show any transition. Note that the critical point of
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}
is independent of the interaction strength
K_{1}
as long as
K_{1}\leq K_{1}^{\mathrm{c}}
. On the other hand, the nature of the criticality depends on the dimension of the system, which we will discuss in the next section. As we will discuss in Sec.
V.5
, to probe the transition in the order parameter
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}
, we will use the quantity
\mathbb{B}\equiv\mathrm{Tr}\big[\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}^{2}\big]
.

V.4
Transition in
\vec{\mathbb{P}}

We now focus on obtaining the evolution equation of
\mathbb{P}
up to second leading order to understand the nature of the transition. We consider the parameter region
K_{2}\leq K_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
and
K_{1}>K_{1}^{\mathrm{c}}
but close to the critical point. If the initial perturbation at
t=0
is small, then the dynamics of
\eta
at initial times will be dominated by
\mathscr{L}
. In this parameter region, the eigenfunctions corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}
is linearly unstable and all other eigenfunctions are linearly stable. Hence, after some time
t
, the leading order contribution to
\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)
will come from the eigenfunctions of
\lambda_{1}
, whereas contribution from the eigenfunctions corresponding to any other eigenvalue
\lambda_{n},n\geq 2
will be sub-leading. During the initial times of the dynamics, we may write

\displaystyle\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)\approx\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}},

(80)

where the components of
\vec{\mathbf{A}}
denote the amplitude along the eigenfunctions of
\lambda_{1}
. At late times, when the components of
\vec{\mathbf{A}}
are sufficiently large,
\mathscr{N}[\eta]
will start contributing to the dynamics of
\eta
. The eigenfunctions with the least negative eigenvalue will contribute in the next leading order. In our case, these eigenfunctions are
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
with the eigenvalue
\lambda_{2}=-2DT
. Hence, in the next leading order, we may write

\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)\approx\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}+\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}.

(81)

Since the contribution from
\mathscr{N}[\eta]
originates as an effect of the leading-order contribution from the most unstable eigenmode, we may assume that
\mathbf{B}
is a function of
\vec{\mathbf{A}}
. Hence, to satisfy the transformation rule given in Eq. (
76
), the matrix
\mathbf{B}
is restricted to have the following form:

\mathbf{B}=b_{0}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{A}}\Big)\mathbf{I}_{D}+b_{1}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{A}}\Big)\vec{\mathbf{A}}\otimes\vec{\mathbf{A}},

(82)

where
b_{0}
and
b_{1}
are scalar functions of
\big|\vec{\mathbf{A}}\big|^{2}
. To preserve the traceless property
\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathbf{B}\big]=0
, we must have
b_{0}\big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{A}}\big)=-D^{-1}\big|\vec{\mathbf{A}}\big|^{2}b_{1}\big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{A}}\big)
.
Further assuming
b_{1}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{A}}\Big)
has a Taylor expansion
b_{1}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{A}}\Big)=b_{10}+b_{12}\big|\vec{\mathbf{A}}\big|^{2}+b_{14}\big|\vec{\mathbf{A}}\big|^{4}+\ldots
, we obtain in the leading order that
\mathbf{B}
can be written as

\mathbf{B}=b_{10}\bigg[\vec{\mathbf{A}}\otimes\vec{\mathbf{A}}-\frac{\big|\vec{\mathbf{A}}\big|^{2}}{D}\mathbf{I}_{D}\bigg]+\ldots,

(83)

where
b_{10}
is to be determined later. Using this in Eq. (
81
), we can express
\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)
entirely in terms of
\vec{\mathbf{A}}
. Our goal now is to obtain an evolution equation for
\vec{\mathbf{A}}
from the evolution equation of
\eta
as given in Eq. (
38
).

Putting Eq. (
81
) back into Eqs. (
39
) and (
40
) along with the results from Appendices
H
and
L
, we obtain

\mathscr{L}\eta=\lambda_{1}\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}+\lambda_{2}~\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}},

(84)

and

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}[\eta]

\displaystyle=K_{1}\Omega_{D}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)^{2}-\frac{2\Omega_{D}}{D}\bigg[K_{1}+\frac{2K_{2}}{(D+2)}\bigg]\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}

\displaystyle-\frac{K_{1}\Omega_{D}}{D}\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{A}}+\frac{4(D+1)K_{2}\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)}\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{(D+1)\Omega_{D}}{D}\bigg[K_{1}+\frac{4K_{2}}{(D+2)}\bigg]\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)

\displaystyle-\frac{8K_{2}\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}^{2}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}.

(85)

The eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
are given in Eq. (
70
).

Taking a derivative with respect to time on both sides of Eq. (
81
), we get

\frac{\partial\eta}{\partial t}=\frac{d\vec{\mathbf{A}}}{dt}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}+\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\frac{d\mathbf{B}}{dt}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}.

(86)

Multiplying both sides of Eq. (
86
) with
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
and integrating over
\mathscr{S}
and using the result from Appendix
L
, we obtain

\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\frac{\partial\eta}{\partial t}~d\Omega=\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D}\frac{d\vec{\mathbf{A}}}{dt},

(87)

which, along with Eq. (
38
), gives

\frac{d\vec{\mathbf{A}}}{dt}=\frac{D}{\Omega_{D}}\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\mathscr{L}\eta~d\Omega+\frac{D}{\Omega_{D}}\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\mathscr{N}[\eta]~d\Omega.

(88)

Computing with the expressions of
\mathscr{L}\eta
and
\mathscr{N}[\eta]
from Eqs. (
84
) and (
85
) and using the results from Appendix
L
, we obtain

\frac{D}{\Omega_{D}}\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\mathscr{L}\eta~d\Omega=\lambda_{1}\vec{\mathbf{A}},

(89)

and

\displaystyle\frac{D}{\Omega_{D}}\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\mathscr{N}[\eta]~d\Omega=-\frac{2\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)}\Bigg[K_{1}-\frac{2D}{(D+2)}K_{2}\Bigg]\mathbf{B}\vec{\mathbf{A}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2(D-1)\Omega_{D}}{D^{2}(D+2)}\Bigg[K_{1}-\frac{2D}{(D+2)}K_{2}\Bigg]b_{10}\big|\vec{\mathbf{A}}\big|^{2}\vec{\mathbf{A}},

(90)

where in obtaining the last equality, we have used the leading order expression of
\mathbf{B}
as given in Eq. (
83
). Putting Eqs. (
89
) and (
90
) back into Eq. (
88
), we obtain

\frac{d\vec{\mathbf{A}}}{dt}=\lambda_{1}\vec{\mathbf{A}}-\frac{2(D-1)\Omega_{D}}{D^{2}(D+2)}\Bigg[K_{1}-\frac{2D}{(D+2)}K_{2}\Bigg]b_{10}\big|\vec{\mathbf{A}}\big|^{2}\vec{\mathbf{A}}.

(91)

Thus, we have obtained the evolution equation of
\vec{\mathbf{A}}
upto the unknown constant
b_{10}
. We now have to compute the coefficient
b_{10}
. Rearranging Eq. (
86
) together with Eq. (
38
), we obtain

\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\frac{d\mathbf{B}}{dt}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}=\mathscr{L}\eta+\mathscr{N}[\eta]-\frac{d\vec{\mathbf{A}}}{dt}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}.

(92)

Using Eqs. (
84
), (
85
) and (
91
) in Eq. (
92
) and keeping terms up to
\mathscr{O}\big(\big|\vec{\mathbf{A}}\big|^{2}\big)
, we obtain

\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\frac{d\mathbf{B}}{dt}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}=\lambda_{2}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}+\Omega_{D}K_{1}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)^{2}-\frac{\Omega_{D}K_{1}}{D}\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{A}},

(93)

where we have used the fact that in leading order,
\mathbf{B}
is
\mathscr{O}\big(\big|\vec{\mathbf{A}}\big|^{2}\big)
, which is evident from Eq. (
83
). Again, using Eq. (
83
), we further obtain, up to leading order, that

\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}=b_{10}\Big[\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)^{2}-\frac{1}{D}\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{A}}\Big]+\ldots.

(94)

Putting this back into Eq. (
93
) we obtain

\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\frac{d\mathbf{B}}{dt}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}=\Big[\lambda_{2}b_{10}+\Omega_{D}K_{1}\Big]\Big[\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)^{2}-\frac{1}{D}\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{A}}\Big].

(95)

Figure 2:
The figure shows numerical-integration results for the stationary state
|\vec{\mathbb{P}}|
, i.e.,
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{st}}
versus
K_{1}
for
D=3
and
4
, respectively for the case with the noise strength
T=1
and
K_{2}=0
. The black dashed line represents the theoretical prediction of the critical points. All simulation data presented in this paper were generated by integrating the equations of motion using a combination of standard fourth-order Runge-Kutta and Euler algorithms, with integration time step equal to
0.001
.

We now focus on computing the left hand side of Eq. (
95
) directly from Eq. (
83
). Taking the time derivative of Eq. (
83
), we obtain

\frac{d\mathbf{B}}{dt}=b_{10}\bigg[\frac{d\vec{\mathbf{A}}}{dt}\otimes\vec{\mathbf{A}}+\vec{\mathbf{A}}\otimes\frac{d\vec{\mathbf{A}}}{dt}-\frac{2}{D}\bigg(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\frac{d\vec{\mathbf{A}}}{dt}\bigg)\mathbf{I}_{D}\bigg]+\ldots.

(96)

Using Eq. (
91
) up to leading order, we obtain

\frac{d\mathbf{B}}{dt}=2\lambda_{1}b_{10}\bigg[\vec{\mathbf{A}}\otimes\vec{\mathbf{A}}-\frac{\big|\vec{\mathbf{A}}\big|^{2}}{D}\mathbf{I}_{D}\bigg]+\ldots,

(97)

which immediately gives

\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\frac{d\mathbf{B}}{dt}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}=2\lambda_{1}b_{10}\bigg[\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)^{2}-\frac{1}{D}\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{A}}\bigg].

(98)

Comparing Eqs. (
95
) and (
98
), we finally get

b_{10}=\frac{\Omega_{D}K_{1}}{2\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}.

(99)

We have thus completely characterized Eq. (
91
). Let us now convert the evolution equation of
\vec{\mathbf{A}}
into the evolution equation of the order parameter
\mathbb{P}=|\vec{\mathbb{P}}|
as defined in Eq. (
30
). Putting Eq. (
36
) along with Eq. (
81
) into Eq. (
30
), we obtain (see Appendix
L
)

\vec{\mathbb{P}}=\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}~\eta\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t\right)d\Omega=\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)d\Omega=\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D}\vec{\mathbf{A}}.

(100)

Substituting this into Eq. (
91
) and taking the inner product with
\vec{\mathbb{P}}
on both sides and noting that
\mathbb{P}^{2}=\vec{\mathbb{P}}\cdot\vec{\mathbb{P}}
, we finally obtain that near criticality, the evolution of the order parameter
\mathbb{P}
satisfies, up to second order, the following equation:

\frac{d\mathbb{P}}{dt}=\lambda_{1}\mathbb{P}-c_{3}\mathbb{P}^{3},

(101)

where we have

\displaystyle c_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{D(D-1)K_{1}}{(D+2)}

\displaystyle\times\left[\frac{(D+2)K_{1}-2DK_{2}}{(D-1)(D+2)K_{1}-2DK_{2}+D(D+2)T}\right].

(102)

Note that Eq. (
101
) has the usual normal form of a pitchfork bifurcation.

At criticality, we have
K_{1}=K_{1}^{\mathrm{c}}=DT
. Putting this into the expression of
c_{3}
, we obtain

c_{3}\big|_{\mathrm{critical}}=\frac{D(D-1)K_{1}}{(D+2)}\left[\frac{(D+2)T-2K_{2}}{D(D+2)T-2K_{2}}\right].

(103)

The parameter region we are interested in here is
K_{2}\leq K_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}=D(D+2)T/2
. From Eq. (
103
), it is clear that in this parameter region of our interest, the denominator of
c_{3}\big|_{\mathrm{critical}}
is always positive. Hence, the sign of
c_{3}\big|_{\mathrm{critical}}
is entirely dependent on its numerator. Explicitly, we have

\displaystyle c_{3}\big|_{\mathrm{critical}}

\displaystyle>0,

\displaystyle\text{when }K_{2}<(D+2)T/2,

(104a)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\text{when }K_{2}=(D+2)T/2,

(104b)

\displaystyle<0,

\displaystyle\text{when }K_{2}>(D+2)T/2.

(104c)

Hence, the order parameter
\mathbb{P}
shows at
K_{1}=K_{1}^{\mathrm{c}}\equiv DT
a continuous transition for
0\leq K_{2}<(D+2)T/2
and a discontinuous transition for
(D+2)T/2\leq K_{2}<D(D+2)T/2
. Hence,
K_{2}=K_{2}^{\mathrm{tc}}\equiv(D+2)/2
is similar to a tri-critical point, around which the system changes its nature of phase transition. The corresponding phase diagram in shown in Fig.
1
.

To close, let us compare our expressions with respect to standard results. For
D=2
, Eq. (
101
) reduces to

\frac{d\mathbb{P}}{dt}=\bigg[\frac{K_{1}}{2}-T\bigg]\mathbb{P}-\frac{K_{1}}{2}\left[\frac{K_{1}-K_{2}}{K_{1}-K_{2}+2T}\right]\mathbb{P}^{3},

(105)

a result reported recently in Ref.
66
, see
Application 1
(note that
\mathbb{P}
is identical to
R_{1}=2\pi|A|
used therein; also, in this reference, the symbol
D
was used to denote the noise strength).

When
K_{2}=0
, at criticality, we have
K_{1}=K_{1}^{\mathrm{c}}=DT
. Putting this into the expression of
c_{3}
, we obtain

c_{3}\big|_{\mathrm{critical}}=\frac{D(D-1)T}{(D+2)}.

(106)

Since
c_{3}\big|_{\mathrm{critical}}>0
for any general dimension
D\geq 2
, we conclude that in the presence of noise and without any quenched disorder, the transition becomes continuous in arbitrary dimensions. This is unlike the noiseless case with quenched disorder as discussed in Ref.
19
, where even and odd dimensions behave differently.

Figure 3:
Figure (a) shows the
N\to\infty
phase diagram of the model defined in Sec.
III
for
D=4
. The red dashed line marks a continuous transition, while the blue solid line indicates a discontinuous transition; at both transitions, the synchronization order parameter
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{st}}
become nonzero simultaneously. The thick blue vertical solid line denotes a discontinuous transition in
\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{st}}
alone, across which
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{st}}
remains zero. Panels (b) and (c) show the behavior of numerically obtained
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{st}}
as a function of
K_{1}
for a continuous case
(K_{2}=2.8)
and a discontinuous case
(K_{2}=3.2)
, respectively. The noise strength is
T=1.0
. The square and the circular markers correspond to
N=10^{3}
and
10^{4}
respectively.

In Figs.
2
and
3
, we demonstrate that our
theoretical predictions for both the critical points (the one for
\mathbb{P}
and the one for
\mathbb{B}
) and the nature of the
associated phase transitions are consistent with simulations for both
odd and even dimensions. Specifically, the
D=3
case is shown in
Fig.
2
(a), while the
D=4
results are shown in
Fig.
2
(b) and Fig.
3
. Figure
2

presents simulation results in the absence of second-harmonic interaction for

D=3
and
D=4
, whereas Fig.
3
illustrates the phase
behavior in the presence of second-harmonic interaction for the representative
case
D=4
. Figure
3
(a) shows the corresponding phase diagram, with
the values of the order parameters in the different phases indicated. In
panel (b), we plot the stationary-state value of the order parameter
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{st}}
as
a function of
K_{1}
for a representative value
K_{2}=2.8
, where the system
undergoes a continuous transition. In contrast, panel (c) shows

\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{st}}
versus
K_{1}
for
K_{2}=3.2
, illustrating a
discontinuous transition. Finally, the dependence of the
order parameter
\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{st}}
on
K_{2}
at fixed
K_{1}
for
different dimensions is shown in Fig.
4
.

We may also compute higher order terms in Eq. (
101
) by considering
\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)
in Eq. (
81
) up to third leading order as

\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)\approx\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}+\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}+\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(3)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes 3}\Big),

(107)

where the symmetric and traceless property, along with the symmetry condition (
77
) requires

\displaystyle T^{(3)}_{ijk}

\displaystyle=h_{0}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{A}}\Big)

\displaystyle\times\bigg\{A_{i}A_{j}A_{k}-\frac{A^{2}}{(D+2)}\Big[\delta_{ij}A_{k}+\delta_{ik}A_{j}+\delta_{jk}A_{i}\Big]\bigg\},

(108)

where
h_{0}(\cdot)
is a scalar function. Following the same procedure as before, we obtain the modification to Eq. (
101
), which reads as

\frac{d\mathbb{P}}{dt}=\lambda_{1}\mathbb{P}-c_{3}\mathbb{P}^{3}-c_{5}\mathbb{P}^{5},

(109)

where the coefficient
c_{5}
reads as

\displaystyle c_{5}

\displaystyle=\frac{2(D-1)\Omega_{D}}{D^{2}(D+2)^{2}}\Bigg\{\bigg[(D+2)K_{1}-2DK_{2}\bigg]b_{12}

\displaystyle+\frac{12DK_{2}}{(D+2)(D+4)}b_{10}h_{10}\Bigg\},

(110)

where we have

\displaystyle h_{10}=\frac{(D+1)\Omega_{D}}{D(3\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3})}\bigg[K_{1}+\frac{4}{(D+2)}K_{2}\bigg]b_{10},

(111)

and

\displaystyle b_{12}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(4\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})}\bigg\{2c_{3}b_{10}+\frac{2\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)(D+4)}

\displaystyle\times\Big[4(D-2)K_{2}b_{10}^{2}-3DK_{1}h_{10}\Big]\bigg\}.

(112)

The coefficients
\lambda_{1},c_{3}
, and
b_{10}
are already given in Eqs. (
70
), (
102
) and (
99
).

V.5
Transition in
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}

In this section, the parameter region we are interested in is
K_{1}\leq K_{1}^{\mathrm{c}}
and
K_{2}>K_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
. In this parameter range, eigenvalue
\lambda_{2}
is positive and the rest of the eigenvalues are negative. Hence, the eigenfunctions corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{2}
become linearly unstable, whereas the rest of the eigenfunctions remain linearly stable. As a result, any perturbation in the eigenspace of
\lambda_{2}
, which has the form
\big(\mathbb{T}^{(2)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)\equiv\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
, will keep on growing with time in the leading order, while perturbation along any other eigenspace will go to zero. If the initial perturbation at
t=0
is small, then the dynamics of
\eta
at initial times will be dominated by
\mathscr{L}
. Hence, after some time
t
, the leading order contribution to
\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)
will come from the eigenfunctions of
\lambda_{2}
, whereas contribution from the eigenfunctions corresponding to any other eigenvalue
\lambda_{n},n\neq 2
will be sub-leading. During the initial times of the dynamics,
\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)
can be written up to leading order as

\displaystyle\eta(\vec{\bm{\sigma}},t)\approx\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}.

(113)

At late times, when the elements of
\mathbf{B}
are sufficiently large,
\mathscr{N}[\eta]
will start contributing to the dynamics of
\eta
. Usually, the eigenfunctions with the least negative eigenvalue will contribute in the next leading order. In our case, these eigenfunctions are either
\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
with the eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}
or
\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(3)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)
with the eigenvalue and
\lambda_{3}
. However, since the contribution from
\mathscr{N}[\eta]
