RM acknowledges useful discussions with Souparna Nath. We acknowledge generous allocation of computational resources of the Department of Theoretical Physics, TIFR, assistance of Kapil Ghadiali and Ajay
Salve, and the financial support of the Department of Atomic Energy,
Government of India under Project Identification No. RTI 4002.

Appendix A
Derivation of Eq. (
20
)

The
lm
-th element
\big[\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{j}(t)\big]_{lm}
of the rotated noise matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{j}(t)
is related to the elements of the original noise matrix
\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)
as

\big[\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{j}(t)\big]_{lm}=\sum_{n}\sum_{p}R_{ln}\big[\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)\big]_{np}R^{\top}_{pm},

(148)

where
R_{ln}
is the
ln
-th element of
\mathbf{R}
,
\big[\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)\big]_{np}=\mathrm{sgn}(n-p)~\eta^{(j)}_{np}(t)
is the
np
-th element of
\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)
and
R^{\top}_{pm}
is the
pm
-th element of
\mathbf{R}^{\top}
. Here,
\mathrm{sgn}(x)
is the standard sign function, or, the signum function. Since
\mathbf{R}
is a constant matrix, we obtain

\big\langle\big[\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{j}(t)\big]_{lm}\big\rangle=\sum_{n}\sum_{p}R_{ln}\big\langle\big[\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)\big]_{np}\big\rangle R^{\top}_{pm}=0,

(149)

since
\big\langle\big[\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)\big]_{np}\big\rangle=\mathrm{sgn}(n-p)\big\langle\eta^{(j)}_{np}(t)\big\rangle=0
. Hence we have

\big\langle\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{j}(t)\big\rangle=\mathbb{O}_{D}.

(150)

Similarly, the
lm
-th element of
\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{i}(t)\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{j}(t^{\prime})=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{N}_{i}(t)\mathbf{R}^{\top}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{N}_{j}(t^{\prime})\mathbf{R}^{\top}=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{N}_{i}(t)\mathbf{N}_{j}(t^{\prime})\mathbf{R}^{\top}
is

\Big[\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{i}(t)\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{j}(t^{\prime})\Big]_{lm}=\sum_{n}\sum_{p}R_{ln}\big[\mathbf{N}_{i}(t)\mathbf{N}_{j}(t^{\prime})\big]_{np}R^{\top}_{pm},

(151)

which, upon averaging over the noise, gives

\Big\langle\Big[\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{i}(t)\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{j}(t^{\prime})\Big]_{lm}\Big\rangle=\sum_{n}\sum_{p}R_{ln}\Big\langle\big[\mathbf{N}_{i}(t)\mathbf{N}_{j}(t^{\prime})\big]_{np}\Big\rangle R^{\top}_{pm}.

(152)

Since for any matrix
\mathbf{B}
, we may write
\big\langle\big[\mathbf{B}\big]_{ab}\big\rangle=\big[\big\langle\mathbf{B}\big\rangle\big]_{ab}
, i.e., the average of the
ab
-th element is equal to the
ab
-th element of the average matrix, we may rewrite the noise terms in Eq. (
152
) as

\displaystyle\Big\langle\big[\mathbf{N}_{i}(t)\mathbf{N}_{j}(t^{\prime})\big]_{np}\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[\Big\langle\mathbf{N}_{i}(t)\mathbf{N}_{j}(t^{\prime})\Big\rangle\Big]_{np}

(153)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(D-1)\delta_{ij}\delta(t-t^{\prime})\big[\mathbf{I}_{D}\big]_{np}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(D-1)\delta_{ij}\delta(t-t^{\prime})\delta_{np},

where in obtaining the last equality, we have used the properties of the noise matrix from Eq. (
15
). Putting Eq. (
153
) back into Eq. (
152
), we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\langle\Big[\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{i}(t)\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{j}(t^{\prime})\Big]_{lm}\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(D-1)\delta_{ij}\delta(t-t^{\prime})\sum_{n}R_{ln}R^{\top}_{nm}

(154)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(D-1)\delta_{ij}\delta(t-t^{\prime})\delta_{lm},

where, in obtaining the last equality, we have used
\sum_{n}R_{ln}R^{\top}_{nm}=\big[\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}^{\top}\big]_{lm}=\big[\mathbf{I}_{D}\big]_{lm}=\delta_{lm}
. Hence we have

\big\langle\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{i}(t)\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{j}(t^{\prime})\big\rangle=-(D-1)\delta_{ij}\delta(t-t^{\prime})\mathbf{I}_{D}.

(155)

Equations (
149
) and (
155
) derived here are the Eq. (
20
) of the main text. We conclude that the properties of the rotated noise matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}_{j}(t)
are identical to those of
\mathbf{N}_{j}(t)
, which are given in Eq. (
15
).

Appendix B
D-dimensional spherical polar coordinate system

In
D
dimensions, let a point be represented in the Cartesian coordinate system as
(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{D})
. In spherical coordinates, the same point may be expressed as
(r,\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\ldots,\theta_{D-2},\phi)
, where we have
\theta_{i}\in[0,\pi]
and
\phi\in[0,2\pi)
. The relation between these coordinates is given by

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r\cos{\theta_{1}},

(156)

\displaystyle x_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r\sin{\theta_{1}}\cos{\theta_{2}},

(157)

\displaystyle x_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r\sin{\theta_{1}}\sin{\theta_{2}}\cos{\theta_{3}},

(158)

\displaystyle x_{D-2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r\sin{\theta_{1}}\sin{\theta_{2}}\cdots\sin{\theta_{D-3}}\cos{\theta_{D-2}},

(160)

\displaystyle x_{D-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r\sin{\theta_{1}}\sin{\theta_{2}}\cdots\sin{\theta_{D-3}}\sin{\theta_{D-2}}\cos{\phi},

(161)

\displaystyle x_{D}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r\sin{\theta_{1}}\sin{\theta_{2}}\cdots\sin{\theta_{D-3}}\sin{\theta_{D-2}}\sin{\phi}.

(162)

Appendix C
Derivation of Fokker-Planck Equation

We start by considering a continuous and differentiable test function
h_{1}(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}(t))
defined on the surface
\mathscr{S}
. Similar to how
F\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}},t\right)
was extended to the entire
\mathbb{R}^{D}
space in Eq. (
26
), we may now extend this function to the entire
\mathbb{R}^{D}
space by defining

\displaystyle h(\vec{\bm{r}}(t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{1}(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}(t))\delta(r-1)

(163)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{1}\left(\frac{\vec{\bm{r}}(t)}{r}\right)\delta(r-1).

As argued in the main text, the dynamics (
25
) will keep the value of any initial
r
constant throughout the evolution. Note that the function
h
has no explicit dependence either on time
t
or on the underlying stochastic evolution. However, since the evolution of
\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t)
is inherently stochastic (see Eq. (
25
)), the value of the function
h
at time
t
will also be random, even when the dynamics of
\vec{\mathbf{r}}
is initiated from a given initial condition. We will be interested in the average of
h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))
over all possible stochastic trajectories of
\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t)
generated by the evolution (
25
); we denote this average by
\langle h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\rangle
. This quantity can also be represented as an average with respect to the distribution of
\vec{\mathbf{r}}
, which reads as

\langle h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\rangle=\int h(\vec{\mathbf{r}})\mathscr{F}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}},t\right)~dV_{D}.

(164)

Taking the time derivative on both sides of Eq. (
164
), we obtain

\left\langle\frac{dh(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))}{dt}\right\rangle=\int h(\vec{\mathbf{r}})\frac{\partial\mathscr{F}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}},t\right)}{\partial t}~dV_{D}.

(165)

Now, using the standard multidimensional Taylor expansion, we obtain

\displaystyle dh(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t)+d\vec{\mathbf{r}})-h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(d\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))+\frac{1}{2!}\left(d\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))+\cdots.

We are interested in terms up to order
dt
from the right hand side of Eq. (
). From Eq. (
25
), the quantity
d\vec{\mathbf{r}}
may be expressed as

d\vec{\mathbf{r}}=\vec{\mathbf{v}}dt+\frac{\sqrt{2T}}{r}~d\mathbf{N}(t)\vec{\mathbf{r}},

(167)

where we have
\vec{\mathbf{v}}=K_{1}\left[\vec{\mathbb{P}}-\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathbb{P}}\right)\vec{\mathbf{r}}/r^{2}\right]+2K_{2}\Big[\mathbb{M}/r-\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\mathbb{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\right)\mathbf{I}_{D}/r^{3}\Big]\vec{\mathbf{r}}
and
d\mathbf{N}(t)=\mathbf{N}(t)dt
. Clearly, in the Cartesian coordinates as in Eq. (
14
), the independent elements of the matrix
d\mathbf{N}(t)
are given by independent Wiener processes, as
\big[d\mathbf{N}(t)\big]_{ij}=dW_{ij}=\eta_{ij}(t)dt~\forall~i<j
, where the quantities
W_{ij}
are Wiener processes. Well-known properties of the Wiener processes give
\langle dW_{ij}dW_{kl}\rangle=\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}dt
, which further implies that
\langle d\mathbf{N}(t)d\mathbf{N}(t)\rangle=-dt(D-1)\mathbf{I}_{D}
.

Let us now focus on the first term on the right hand side of the second equality in Eq. (
). Its average with respect to the stochastic noise gives

\displaystyle\left\langle\left(d\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\left(\vec{\mathbf{v}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\right\rangle dt+\sqrt{2T}\left\langle\frac{1}{r}\left(d\mathbf{N}(t)\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\right\rangle,

(168)

where we have used Eq. (
167
). In the Itô interpretation of stochastic differential equations, the Wiener increment
d\mathbf{N}(t)
at time
t
is statistically independent of the state
\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t)
. Hence, we may write

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{1}{r}\left(d\mathbf{N}(t)\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{1}{r}\left(\left\langle d\mathbf{N}(t)\right\rangle\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\right\rangle

(169)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

where in obtaining the first equality, we perform the averaging in two steps, by first fixing the value of
\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t)
and averaging over
d\mathbf{N}(t)
, and finally averaging over
\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t)
. Equation (
168
) then simplifies to

\left\langle\left(d\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\right\rangle=\left\langle\left(\vec{\mathbf{v}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\right\rangle dt.

(170)

Let us now turn to the second term in the second equality on the right hand side of Eq. (
). Using Eq. (
167
), the only
\mathscr{O}(dt)
contribution from that term is then obtained as

\frac{1}{2!}\left(d\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\approx T\left(\frac{1}{r}~d\mathbf{N}(t)\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t)).

(171)

Using vector calculus, this expression can be further simplified (see Appendix
D
); we get

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{r}d\mathbf{N}(t)\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r^{2}}d\mathbf{N}(t)d\mathbf{N}(t)\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[d\mathbf{N}(t)\left\{\vec{\nabla}\otimes\vec{\nabla}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\right\}d\mathbf{N}(t)\left\{\vec{\mathbf{r}}\otimes\vec{\mathbf{r}}\right\}\right].

(172)

Performing the average of Eq. (
172
) with respect to the stochastic noise gives

\displaystyle\left\langle\left(\frac{1}{r}d\mathbf{N}(t)\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left\langle d\mathbf{N}(t)d\mathbf{N}(t)\right\rangle\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\right\rangle

\displaystyle-\left\langle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[d\mathbf{N}(t)\left\{\vec{\nabla}\otimes\vec{\nabla}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\right\}d\mathbf{N}(t)\left\{\vec{\mathbf{r}}\otimes\vec{\mathbf{r}}\right\}\right]\right\rangle.

(173)

Using the properties of
d\mathbf{N}(t)
, we may simplify Eq. (
173
) into (see Appendix
E
)

\displaystyle\left\langle\left(\frac{1}{r}d\mathbf{N}(t)\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\right\rangle

\displaystyle=dt\left\langle\nabla^{2}h\right\rangle-dt(D-1)\left\langle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h\right\rangle-dt~\left\langle\left(\hat{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h\right\rangle.

(174)

We now express the gradient operator
\vec{\nabla}
in terms of its radial and angular components on the sphere
\mathscr{S}
as

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}=\hat{\mathbf{r}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+\frac{1}{r}\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}},

(175)

where
\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}
is the surface divergence on the surface of the unit sphere
\mathscr{S}
in
D
dimensions. Clearly,
\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}=0.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial h}{\partial r}\right\rangle,

(176)

\displaystyle\left\langle\left(\hat{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\frac{\partial^{2}h}{\partial r^{2}}\right\rangle,

(177)

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla^{2}h\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\frac{\partial^{2}h}{\partial r^{2}}+\frac{D-1}{r}\frac{\partial h}{\partial r}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}h\right\rangle,

(178)

where
\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}
is the Laplace-Beltrami operator on
\mathscr{S}
. For a proof of Eq. (
178
), see Appendix
F
. Hence, Eq. (
174
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\left\langle\left(\frac{1}{r}d\mathbf{N}(t)\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\right\rangle=dt\left\langle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}h\right\rangle.

(179)

Putting Eqs. (
170
), (
171
) and (
179
) back into Eq. (
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{dh(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))}{dt}\right\rangle=\left\langle\left(\vec{\mathbf{v}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\right\rangle+\left\langle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\right\rangle.

(180)

Using the same argument as the one used to obtain Eq. (
164
), we may also write the first term on the right hand side of Eq. (
180
) as

\displaystyle\left\langle\left(\vec{\mathbf{v}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\mathscr{F}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}},t\right)\vec{\mathbf{v}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}})~dV_{D}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int h(\vec{\mathbf{r}})\vec{\nabla}\cdot\left[\mathscr{F}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}},t\right)\vec{\mathbf{v}}\right]~dV_{D},

where in the last step, we have performed integration by parts and have taken the boundary contributions to vanish. Similarly, the second term on the right hand side of Eq. (
180
) may also be simplified as

\left\langle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\right\rangle=\int\mathscr{F}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}},t\right)\frac{1}{r^{2}}\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}})~dV_{D}.

(182)

Using the definition of
h
from Eq. (
163
) in Eq. (
182
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{r^{2}}\delta(r-1)\left[\int\mathscr{F}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}},t\right)\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}h_{1}(\vec{\bm{\sigma}})~d\Omega\right]r^{D-1}dr

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{r^{2}}\delta(r-1)\left[\int h_{1}(\vec{\bm{\sigma}})\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}\mathscr{F}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}},t\right)~d\Omega\right]r^{D-1}dr

\displaystyle=\int h(\vec{\mathbf{r}})\left[\frac{1}{r^{2}}\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}\mathscr{F}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}},t\right)\right]dV_{D},

(183)

where in obtaining the second equality, we have performed integration by parts for the term within the square brackets.

Using Eqs. (
165
) and (
180
) along with Eqs. (
) and (
183
) and noting that
h
is an arbitrary function on
\mathscr{S}
, we arrive at the desired Fokker-Planck equation, which reads as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathscr{F}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\vec{\nabla}\cdot\left[\mathscr{F}\vec{\mathbf{v}}\right]+\frac{T}{r^{2}}\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}\mathscr{F}.

(184)

Equation (
184
) is the desired Eq. (
28
) of the main text.

Appendix D
Derivation of Eq. (
172
)

Here, we prove the expression given in Eq. (
172
). The left hand side is of the form
\left(r^{-1}\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}})
, where
\mathbf{M}
is a constant (
\vec{\mathbf{r}}
-independent) antisymmetric matrix. The given quantity rewrites as

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{r}\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}})=\frac{1}{r}\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\left[\frac{1}{r}\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h\right].

(185)

Using the vector calculus identity
\vec{\nabla}(fg)=f\vec{\nabla}g+g\vec{\nabla}f
for any two scalar functions
f
and
g
, we may simplify Eq. (
185
) into

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\left[\frac{1}{r}\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h\right)\vec{\nabla}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)+\frac{1}{r}\vec{\nabla}\left[\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{r^{3}}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\left(\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h\right)+\frac{1}{r}\vec{\nabla}\left[\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h\right].

Using this in Eq. (
185
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{r}\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{r^{4}}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\left(\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h\right)\left(\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{r}}\right)

(187)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}).

Since
\mathbf{M}
is an antisymmetric matrix, we have
\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{r}}=0
. Hence, Eq. (
187
) reduces to

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{r}\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}})=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}).

(188)

Let us now focus on computing
\left(\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}})
. We will do further computations in the Cartesian coordinate system. Let
M_{ij}
denote the
ij
-th element of the matrix
\mathbf{M}
. Similarly, let the
i
-th component of the vector
\vec{\mathbf{r}}
be
r_{i}
. Hence, we may write
\vec{\mathbf{r}}=\sum_{i=1}^{D}x_{i}\hat{\mathbf{r}}_{i}
, where
\hat{\mathbf{r}}_{i}
is the unit vector along the
i
-th Cartesian coordinate axis. Using this, we may write

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}})=\sum_{k=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}M_{kj}x_{j}\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_{k}}.

(189)

Proceeding in a similar way, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\left[\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{D}\sum_{m=1}^{D}M_{lm}x_{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{l}}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}M_{kj}x_{j}\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_{k}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{D}\sum_{m=1}^{D}\sum_{k=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}\delta_{lj}M_{lm}M_{kj}x_{m}\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_{k}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{l=1}^{D}\sum_{m=1}^{D}\sum_{k=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}M_{lm}M_{kj}x_{m}x_{j}\frac{\partial^{2}h}{\partial x_{l}\partial x_{k}}.

(190)

Now, the first term may be simplified as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{D}\sum_{m=1}^{D}\sum_{k=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}\delta_{lj}M_{lm}M_{kj}x_{m}\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{D}\sum_{k=1}^{D}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{D}M_{kj}M_{jm}\right)x_{m}\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{D}\sum_{m=1}^{D}\left(\mathbf{M}^{2}\right)_{km}x_{m}\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_{k}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{M}^{2}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}).

(191)

Using the anti-symmetry property of the
\mathbf{M}
matrix, the second term simplifies to

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{D}\sum_{m=1}^{D}\sum_{k=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}M_{lm}M_{kj}x_{m}x_{j}\frac{\partial^{2}h}{\partial x_{l}\partial x_{k}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{m=1}^{D}\sum_{l=1}^{D}\sum_{k=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}M_{ml}\left[\frac{\partial^{2}h}{\partial x_{l}\partial x_{k}}\right]M_{kj}\left[x_{j}x_{m}\right]

\displaystyle=-\sum_{m=1}^{D}\sum_{l=1}^{D}\sum_{k=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}\mathbf{M}_{ml}\left[\vec{\nabla}\otimes\vec{\nabla}h\right]_{lk}\mathbf{M}_{kj}\left[\vec{\mathbf{r}}\otimes\vec{\mathbf{r}}\right]_{jm}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbf{M}\left(\vec{\nabla}\otimes\vec{\nabla}h\right)\mathbf{M}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}}\otimes\vec{\mathbf{r}}\right)\right].

(192)

Hence, combining Eqs. (
190
), (
191
) and (
192
), we finally obtain

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{M}^{2}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}})-\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbf{M}\left(\vec{\nabla}\otimes\vec{\nabla}h\right)\mathbf{M}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}}\otimes\vec{\mathbf{r}}\right)\right].

(193)

Using this in Eq. (
188
), we finally obtain

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{r}\mathbf{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\mathbf{M}^{2}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}})-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbf{M}\left(\vec{\nabla}\otimes\vec{\nabla}h\right)\mathbf{M}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}}\otimes\vec{\mathbf{r}}\right)\right],

which is used to obtain Eq. (
172
) of Appendix
C
.

Appendix E
Derivation of Eq. (
174
)

The independent matrix elements of
d\mathbf{N}(t)
, represented by
\left[d\mathbf{N}(t)\right]_{ij}
, are independent differential Wiener processes. We represent them by
\left[d\mathbf{N}(t)\right]_{ij}=-\left[d\mathbf{N}(t)\right]_{ji}=dW_{ij}~\forall~i>j
. This further gives us

\displaystyle\left\langle\left[d\mathbf{N}(t)\right]_{ij}\left[d\mathbf{N}(t)\right]_{kl}\right\rangle=dt\left(\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}-\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}\right).

(195)

Using
\langle d\mathbf{N}(t)d\mathbf{N}(t)\rangle=-dt(D-1)\mathbf{I}
in the first term on the right hand side of Eq. (
173
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left\langle d\mathbf{N}(t)d\mathbf{N}(t)\right\rangle\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h\right\rangle=-dt(D-1)\left\langle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h\right\rangle.

(196)

We now focus on the second term on the right hand side of Eq. (
173
). We will do all further computations in the Cartesian coordinate system. Using Eq. (
195
) in the second term, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[d\mathbf{N}(t)\left(\vec{\nabla}\otimes\vec{\nabla}h\right)d\mathbf{N}(t)\left(\vec{\mathrm{r}}\otimes\vec{\mathrm{r}}\right)\right]\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{D}\sum_{l=1}^{D}\sum_{k=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}\left\langle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left[d\mathbf{N}(t)\right]_{ml}\frac{\partial^{2}h}{\partial\sigma_{l}\partial\sigma_{k}}\left[d\mathbf{N}(t)\right]_{kj}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{m}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\sum_{l=1}^{D}\sum_{m=1}^{D}\sum_{k=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}\left\langle\left[d\mathbf{N}(t)\right]_{lm}\left[d\mathbf{N}(t)\right]_{kj}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{\sigma_{m}\sigma_{j}}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}h}{\partial\sigma_{l}\partial\sigma_{k}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-dt\sum_{l=1}^{D}\sum_{m=1}^{D}\sum_{k=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}\left(\delta_{lk}\delta_{mj}-\delta_{lj}\delta_{mk}\right)\left\langle\frac{\sigma_{m}\sigma_{j}}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}h}{\partial\sigma_{l}\partial\sigma_{k}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-dt\sum_{l=1}^{D}\sum_{m=1}^{D}\left[\left\langle\frac{\sigma_{m}^{2}}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}h}{\partial\sigma_{l}^{2}}\right\rangle-\left\langle\frac{\sigma_{m}\sigma_{l}}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}h}{\partial\sigma_{l}\partial\sigma_{m}}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=-dt\ \left\langle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{D}\sigma_{m}^{2}\right)\sum_{l=1}^{D}\frac{\partial^{2}h}{\partial\sigma_{l}^{2}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle~~~~+dt\left\langle\left(\frac{1}{r}\sum_{m=1}^{D}\sigma_{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma_{m}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{r}\sum_{l=1}^{D}\sigma_{l}\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma_{l}}\right)h\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-dt~\left\langle\frac{1}{r^{2}}r^{2}\nabla^{2}h\right\rangle+dt~\left\langle\left(\frac{1}{r}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-dt~\left\langle\nabla^{2}h\right\rangle+dt~\left\langle\left(\hat{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h\right\rangle.

(197)

In the semifinal step, we have used
r^{2}=\sum_{m=1}^{D}\sigma_{m}^{2}
. Using Eqs. (
196
) and (
197
) in Eq. (
173
), we finally obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{1}{r}\left(d\mathbf{N}(t)\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h(\vec{\mathbf{r}}(t))\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-dt~(D-1)\left\langle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h\right\rangle-dt\left\langle\left(\hat{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\right)^{2}h\right\rangle+dt\left\langle\nabla^{2}h\right\rangle,

which is Eq. (
174
) of the main text.

Appendix F
Derivation of Eq. (
178
)

We start with the expression of
\vec{\nabla}
in
D
-dimensional spherical polar coordinates, which reads as

\vec{\nabla}=\hat{\mathbf{r}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+\frac{1}{r}\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}.

(199)

Now, our quantity of interest may be evaluated as follows:

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}h

\displaystyle=\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{\nabla}h=\vec{\nabla}\cdot\bigg[\hat{\mathbf{r}}\frac{\partial h}{\partial r}\bigg]+\vec{\nabla}\cdot\bigg[\frac{1}{r}\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}h\bigg]

\displaystyle=\big(\vec{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{r}}\big)\frac{\partial h}{\partial r}+\hat{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\bigg[\frac{\partial h}{\partial r}\bigg]+\vec{\nabla}\bigg[\frac{1}{r}\bigg]\cdot\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}h+\frac{1}{r}\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}h

\displaystyle=\frac{D-1}{r}\frac{\partial h}{\partial r}+\frac{\partial^{2}h}{\partial r^{2}}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}h.

(200)

In the last step, we have used the fact that
\hat{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}=0
. Also, we have used
\vec{\nabla}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{r}}=(D-1)/r
. Equation (
200
) is Eq. (
178
) of Appendix
C
.

Appendix G
Derivation of Eq. (
29
)

We start with the first term on the right hand side of Eq. (
28
), which upon using standard vector identity gives

\vec{\nabla}\cdot\left[\mathscr{F}\vec{\mathbf{v}}\right]=\vec{\mathbf{v}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\mathscr{F}+\mathscr{F}\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{v}},

(201)

where we have
\vec{\mathbf{v}}=K_{1}\left[\vec{\mathbb{P}}-\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathbb{P}}\right)\vec{\mathbf{r}}/r^{2}\right]+2K_{2}\Bigg[\mathbb{M}/r-\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\mathbb{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\right)\mathbf{I}_{D}/r^{3}\Bigg]\vec{\mathbf{r}}
. Now, using Eq. (
175
), we have

\vec{\nabla}\mathscr{F}=\hat{\mathbf{r}}\frac{\partial\mathscr{F}}{\partial r}+\frac{1}{r}\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}\mathscr{F}.

(202)

Since
\vec{\mathbf{v}}
is along the tangent to the sphere centered at the origin, we have
\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{v}}=0
. This is also evident from the expression of
\vec{\mathbf{v}}
on using the result
\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{r}}=r^{2}
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle\vec{\mathbf{v}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\mathscr{F}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}\vec{\mathbf{v}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}\mathscr{F}

(203)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}\Big(K_{1}\vec{\mathbb{P}}+2K_{2}\mathbb{M}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)\cdot\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}\mathscr{F}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta(r-1)}{r}\bigg[\Big(K_{1}\vec{\mathbb{P}}+2K_{2}\mathbb{M}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)\cdot\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}F\bigg],

where in deriving the second equality, we have used
\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}\mathscr{F}=0
by the definition of
\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}\mathscr{F}
.

We now focus on the second term on the right hand side of Eq. (
201
). Using the expression of
\vec{\mathbf{v}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{v}}

\displaystyle=K_{1}\vec{\nabla}\cdot\Bigg[\vec{\mathbb{P}}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathbb{P}}\right)\vec{\mathbf{r}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle+2K_{2}\vec{\nabla}\cdot\Bigg[\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{M\vec{\mathbf{r}}}-\frac{1}{r^{3}}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\mathbb{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\right)\vec{\mathbf{r}}\Bigg].

(204)

Focusing on the first term on the right hand side of Eq. (
204
),
in terms of Cartesian components, we may write

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\Bigg[\vec{\mathbb{P}}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathbb{P}}\right)\vec{\mathbf{r}}\Bigg]

(205)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\vec{\nabla}\cdot\Big[\left(\vec{\mathbb{P}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bigg[\left(\vec{\mathbb{P}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}+\vec{\nabla}\left(\vec{\mathbb{P}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bigg[\left(\vec{\mathbb{P}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)\bigg[\frac{D-1}{r}\bigg]+\frac{1}{r}\Big[\vec{\mathbb{P}}-\left(\vec{\mathbb{P}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big]\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{r}(D-1)\vec{\mathbb{P}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}.

Here, in obtaining the fourth equality, we have used the identity
\vec{\nabla}\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)=\frac{1}{r}\Big[\vec{\mathbf{A}}-\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big]
for any constant vector
\vec{\mathbf{A}}
, which we derive below (see Eq. (
215
)).

We now focus on simplifying the second bracketed term in Eq. (
204
). From Eq. (
223
), we obtain

\vec{\nabla}\cdot\Bigg[\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\Bigg]=\frac{1}{r}\bigg[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathbb{M}\big]-\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbb{M}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\bigg],

(206)

and from Eq. (
232
), we obtain

\vec{\nabla}\cdot\Bigg[\frac{1}{r^{3}}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\mathbb{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\right)\vec{\mathbf{r}}\Bigg]=\frac{(D-1)}{r}\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbb{M}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right).

(207)

Combining these results, we obtain

\vec{\nabla}\cdot\Bigg[\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{M\vec{\mathbf{r}}}-\frac{1}{r^{3}}\left(\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\mathbb{M}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\right)\vec{\mathbf{r}}\Bigg]=\frac{1}{r}\bigg[1-D\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbb{M}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\bigg],

(208)

where we have used the fact that
\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathbb{M}\big]=1
. Hence, from Eq. (
204
), we get

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{v}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\delta(r-1)}{r}K_{1}(D-1)F~\vec{\mathbb{P}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\delta(r-1)}{r}2K_{2}F-\frac{\delta(r-1)}{r}2K_{2}DF~\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbb{M}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}.

(209)

Using Eqs. (
26
), (
201
), (
203
), and (
209
) in Eq. (
28
), we obtain

\displaystyle\delta(r-1)\frac{\partial F}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\delta(r-1)}{r}\bigg[\Big(K_{1}\vec{\mathbb{P}}+2K_{2}\mathbb{M}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)\cdot\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}F\bigg]

\displaystyle+\frac{\delta(r-1)}{r}K_{1}(D-1)F~\vec{\mathbb{P}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\delta(r-1)}{r}2K_{2}DF~\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbb{M}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\delta(r-1)}{r}2K_{2}F+\frac{\delta(r-1)}{r^{2}}T\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}F.

(210)

Integrating both sides over
r
from
1-\epsilon
to
1+\epsilon
for small
\epsilon
, we finally obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F}{\partial t}=

\displaystyle-K_{1}\left[\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}F-(D-1)F\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right]\cdot\vec{\mathbb{P}}-2K_{2}F

\displaystyle-2K_{2}\left[\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}F-DF\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right]\cdot\mathbb{M}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}+T~\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}F.

(211)

Equation (
211
) is the desired Eq. (
29
) of the main text.

Appendix H
Derivation of some useful identities

We start from the following expression:

\vec{\nabla}\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)=\vec{\nabla}\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\frac{\vec{\mathbf{r}}}{r}\right).

(212)

Now, in the Cartesian coordinates, if we denote the
j
-th component of
\vec{\mathbf{A}}
by
A_{j}
and that of
\vec{\mathbf{r}}
by
\sigma_{j}
, then we may write

\vec{\nabla}\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)=\vec{\nabla}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{D}\frac{A_{j}\sigma_{j}}{r}\right).

(213)

Hence, the
k
-th component of
\vec{\nabla}\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)
may be written as

\displaystyle\Big[\vec{\nabla}\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)\Big]_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma_{k}}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{D}\frac{A_{j}\sigma_{j}}{r}\right)

(214)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{D}\frac{A_{j}\delta_{jk}}{r}-\sum_{j=1}^{D}\frac{A_{j}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}}{r^{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{A_{k}}{r}-\frac{\sigma_{k}}{r^{2}}\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right).

Hence, we may write

\vec{\nabla}\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)=\frac{1}{r}\Big[\vec{\mathbf{A}}-\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big].

(215)

Now, from Eq. (
175
), we may rewrite the left hand side of the equation as

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)=\Big[\hat{\mathbf{r}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+\frac{1}{r}\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}\Big]\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right).

(216)

Since
\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
is independent of
r
, we may immediately write

\vec{\nabla}\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)=\frac{1}{r}\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right).

(217)

Comparing Eqs. (
215
) and (
217
), we finally obtain

\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)=\vec{\mathbf{A}}-\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}.

(218)

Taking divergence on both sides of Eq. (
218
), we obtain

\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)=-\vec{\nabla}\cdot\Big[\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big],

(219)

where we have used
\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{A}}=0
, since
\vec{\mathbf{A}}
is a constant vector. The left hand side of Eq. (
219
) gives

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[\hat{\mathbf{r}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+\frac{1}{r}\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}\Big]\cdot\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)

(220)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right),

where we have used
\hat{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}=0
. Let us now simplify the right hand side of Eq. (
219
). Using standard vector identity, we obtain

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\Big[\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}+\vec{\nabla}\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}

\displaystyle=\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)\bigg[\frac{D-1}{r}\bigg]+\frac{1}{r}\Big[\vec{\mathbf{A}}-\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big]\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r}(D-1)\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}},

(221)

where in obtaining the second equality, we have used Eq. (
215
), while in arriving at the last step of Eq. (
221
), we have used
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}=1
which makes the second term on the right hand side of the second equality equal to zero. Putting Eqs. (
220
) and (
221
) back into Eq. (
219
), we finally obtain

\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}\left(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)=-(D-1)\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}.

(222)

Another expression we want to compute is

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{D}\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma_{j}}\bigg[\sum_{m=1}^{D}B_{jm}\bigg(\frac{\sigma_{m}}{r}\bigg)\bigg]

(223)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{D}\sum_{m=1}^{D}B_{jm}\bigg[\frac{\delta_{jm}}{r}-\frac{\sigma_{m}\sigma_{j}}{r^{3}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}\bigg[\sum_{m=1}^{D}B_{mm}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\sum_{m=1}^{D}\sigma_{j}\sum_{m=1}^{D}B_{jm}\sigma_{m}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}\bigg[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathbf{B}\big]-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\mathbf{r}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}\bigg[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathbf{B}\big]-\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\bigg].

We now present another computation of interest:

\vec{\nabla}\big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)=\vec{\nabla}\bigg(\mathbf{B}\frac{\vec{\mathbf{r}}}{r}\cdot\frac{\vec{\mathbf{r}}}{r}\bigg).

(224)

Now, in the Cartesian coordinates, if we denote the
j
-th component of
\vec{\mathbf{r}}
by
\sigma_{j}
and
ij
-th element of
\mathbf{B}
by
B_{ij}
, then we may write

\vec{\nabla}\big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)=\vec{\nabla}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}B_{ij}\frac{\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}{r^{2}}\bigg).

(225)

If we focus on the
k
-th component of
\vec{\nabla}\big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Big[\vec{\nabla}\big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)\Big]_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma_{k}}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}B_{ij}\frac{\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}{r^{2}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}B_{ij}\delta_{ik}\frac{\sigma_{j}}{r^{2}}+\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}B_{ij}\delta_{jk}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{r^{2}}

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}B_{ij}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\frac{2\sigma_{k}}{r^{4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}B_{kj}\frac{\sigma_{j}}{r}+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}B_{ik}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{r}

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{r}\bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{D}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{r}\sum_{j=1}^{D}B_{ij}\frac{\sigma_{j}}{r}\bigg]\frac{\sigma_{k}}{r}.

(226)

Since
\mathbf{B}
is a symmetric matrix, we may simplify the previous expression as

\displaystyle\Big[\vec{\nabla}\big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)\Big]_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}B_{jk}\frac{\sigma_{j}}{r}+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}B_{ki}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{r}

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{r}\bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{D}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{r}\sum_{j=1}^{D}B_{ij}\frac{\sigma_{j}}{r}\bigg]\frac{\sigma_{k}}{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{r}\bigg[\mathbf{B}\frac{\vec{\mathbf{r}}}{r}\bigg]_{k}-\frac{2}{r}\bigg(\frac{\vec{\mathbf{r}}}{r}\cdot\mathbf{B}\frac{\vec{\mathbf{r}}}{r}\bigg)\bigg[\frac{\vec{\mathbf{r}}}{r}\bigg]_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{r}\bigg[\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}-\big(\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\bigg]_{k}.

(227)

Hence, we may write

\vec{\nabla}\big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)=\frac{2}{r}\bigg[\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}-\big(\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\bigg].

(228)

Now, from Eq. (
175
), we may rewrite the left hand side of the equation as

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)=\Big[\hat{\mathbf{r}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+\frac{1}{r}\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}\Big]\big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big).

(229)

Since
\big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)
is independent of
r
, we may immediately write

\vec{\nabla}\big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)=\frac{1}{r}\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}\big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big).

(230)

Comparing Eqs. (
228
) and (
230
), we finally obtain

\vec{\nabla}_{\mathscr{S}}\big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)=2\bigg[\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}-\big(\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\bigg].

(231)

Another quantity we want to compute is

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\Bigg[\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Bigg]=\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}\Big[\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big]+\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}

\displaystyle=\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\bigg[\frac{2}{r}\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}-\frac{2}{r}\big(\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\bigg]+\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right)\frac{(D-1)}{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{(D-1)}{r}\left(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\right).

(232)

Appendix I
Derivation of Eq. (
66
)

In the Cartesian coordinates in
D
dimensions, we may write a general position vector
\vec{\mathbf{r}}=r\vec{\bm{\sigma}}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{D})
and the corresponding unit vector
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{D})
as defined in Sec.
V.2
. Using this, we may define a new function

\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\mathbf{r}}^{\otimes n}\Big)\equiv\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{D}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{D}\cdots\sum_{i_{n}=1}^{D}T^{(n)}_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n}}x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}\ldots x_{i_{n}},

(233)

where
\mathbb{T}^{(n)}
is a symmetric traceless tensor of order
n
. Since
x_{j}=r\sigma_{j}
for any
j=1,2,\dots,D
, we may write

\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\mathbf{r}}^{\otimes n}\Big)=r^{n}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big),

(234)

where
\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big)
is defined in Eq. (
64
).

We now study the effect of the full Laplacian
\nabla^{2}
on
\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\mathbf{r}}^{\otimes n}\Big)
. We first compute the
k
-th component of
\vec{\nabla}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\mathbf{r}}^{\otimes n}\Big)
in the Cartesian coordinates, which reads as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\mathbf{r}}^{\otimes n}\Big)=\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{D}\cdots\sum_{i_{n}=1}^{D}T^{(n)}_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n}}\Big[\delta_{i_{1},k}x_{i_{2}}\ldots x_{i_{n}}

\displaystyle+x_{i_{1}}\delta_{i_{2},k}x_{i_{3}}\ldots x_{i_{n}}+\cdots+x_{i_{1}}\ldots x_{i_{n-1}}\delta_{i_{n},k}\Big].

(235)

Since
\mathbb{T}^{(n)}
is a symmetric tensor, we may simplify Eq. (
235
) into

\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\mathbf{r}}^{\otimes n}\Big)=n\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{D}\cdots\sum_{i_{n}=1}^{D}T^{(n)}_{k,i_{2},i_{3},\ldots,i_{n}}x_{i_{2}}x_{i_{3}}\ldots x_{i_{n}}.

(236)

Repeating the same argument, we obtain

\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}_{k}}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\mathbf{r}}^{\otimes n}\Big)=n(n-1)\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{D}\cdots\sum_{i_{n}=1}^{D}T^{(n)}_{k,k,i_{3},\ldots,i_{n}}x_{i_{3}}\ldots x_{i_{n}}.

(237)

Now computing
\nabla^{2}\Big\langle\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\mathbf{r}}^{\otimes n}\Big\rangle
in the Cartesian components, we obtain

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\mathbf{r}}^{\otimes n}\Big)=\sum_{k=1}^{D}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}_{k}}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\mathbf{r}}^{\otimes n}\Big),

\displaystyle=n(n-1)\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{D}\cdots\sum_{i_{n}=1}^{D}\bigg[\sum_{k=1}^{D}T^{(n)}_{k,k,i_{3},\ldots,i_{n}}\bigg]x_{i_{3}}\ldots x_{i_{n}}=0,

(238)

where in the last step, we have used the traceless property of
\mathbb{T}^{(n)}
as given in Eq. (
65
).

We now compute
\nabla^{2}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\mathbf{r}}^{\otimes n}\Big)
in
D
-dimensional spherical-polar coordinates as defined in Appendix
B
. Using Eq. (
200
) along with Eq. (
234
) and noting the fact that
\Big\langle\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big\rangle
is independent of
r
, we obtain

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\mathbf{r}}^{\otimes n}\Big),

\displaystyle=\bigg[\frac{D-1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial r^{2}}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}\bigg]\bigg[r^{n}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big)\bigg],

\displaystyle=r^{n-2}\bigg[n(n+D-2)\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big)+\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big)\bigg].

(239)

Comparing Eqs. (
238
) and (
239
), we obtain

\nabla^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big)=-n(n+D-2)\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big).

(240)

Equation (
240
) is the desired Eq. (
66
) of the main text.

Appendix J
Derivation of Eq. (
67
)

Using the definition of
\big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\big)
from Eq. (
64
), we may write the
k
-th component of the integral
\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\big)d\Omega
as

\displaystyle\bigg[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big)d\Omega\bigg]_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{D}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{D}\cdots\sum_{i_{n}=1}^{D}T^{(n)}_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n}}\int\sigma_{k}\sigma_{i_{1}}\sigma_{i_{2}}\ldots\sigma_{i_{n}}d\Omega.

(241)

If
n
is even, then the integral contains the product of odd number of
\sigma
’s. Hence, using Eq. (
246
), we immediately get
\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big)d\Omega=0
when
n
is even.

When
n
is odd and
n\geq 3
, we compute integrals of product of even number of
\sigma_{i}
’s. Using Eq. (
246
), we obtain

\displaystyle\bigg[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big)d\Omega\bigg]_{k}=\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{D}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{D}\cdots\sum_{i_{n}=1}^{D}T^{(n)}_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n}}

\displaystyle\times\bigg[\frac{\Omega_{D}}{\prod_{k=0}^{m-1}(D+2k)}\sum_{\{\mathrm{pairings}~P\}}\prod_{(a,b)\in P}\delta_{i_{a}i_{b}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Omega_{D}}{\prod_{k=0}^{m-1}(D+2k)}\sum_{\mathrm{pairings}~P}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{D}\cdots\sum_{i_{\alpha-1}=1}^{D}\sum_{i_{\alpha+1}=1}^{D}\cdots\sum_{i_{\beta-1}=1}^{D}

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{\beta+1}=1}^{D}\cdots\sum_{i_{n}=1}^{D}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}(a,b)\in P\\
(a,b)\neq(\alpha,\beta)\end{subarray}}\delta_{i_{a}i_{b}}\bigg[\sum_{i_{\alpha}=1}^{D}\sum_{i_{\beta}=1}^{D}\delta_{i_{\alpha}i_{\beta}}

\displaystyle\times T^{(n)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\alpha},\ldots,i_{\beta},\ldots,i_{n}}\bigg].

(242)

Now the term inside the bracket in the last step is a trace of
\mathbb{T}^{(n)}
, and from Eq. (
65
), we know it is zero since
\mathbb{T}^{(n)}
is a traceless tensor. Hence,
\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\otimes n}\Big)d\Omega=0
when
n
is odd and
n\geq 3
.

The only case left to compute is
n=1
. Focusing on the
k
-th component and using Eq. (
246
), we obtain

\displaystyle\bigg[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(1)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)d\Omega\bigg]_{k}=\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{D}T^{(1)}_{i_{1}}\int\sigma_{k}\sigma_{i_{1}}d\Omega

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{D}T^{(1)}_{i_{1}}\bigg[\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D}\delta_{i_{1}k}\bigg]=\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D}T^{(1)}_{k},

(243)

which immediately gives

\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(1)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\Big)d\Omega^{\prime}=\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D}\sum_{k=1}^{D}T^{(1)}_{k}\sigma_{k}=\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(1)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big).

(244)

Combining everything, we thus obtain

\displaystyle\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\Big)d\Omega^{\prime}=\delta_{n,1}\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D}\Big(\mathbb{T}^{(n)},\vec{\bm{\sigma}}^{\prime}\Big).

(245)

Equation (
245
) is the desired Eq. (
67
) of the main text.

Appendix K
Tensor Integral Formula

Let
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}
be a
D
-dimensional unit vector having the form
\vec{\bm{\sigma}}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{D})
in Cartesian coordinates, then it is known that
101
;
17
;
102

\displaystyle\int\sigma_{i_{1}}\sigma_{i_{2}}\ldots\sigma_{i_{n}}~d\Omega

\displaystyle=0,~~~\mathrm{if}~n=2m+1,

\displaystyle=\frac{\Omega_{D}}{\prod_{k=0}^{m-1}(D+2k)}\sum_{\{\mathrm{pairings}\,P\}}\prod_{(a,b)\in P}\delta_{i_{a}i_{b}},~~~\mathrm{if}~n=2m.

(246)

Appendix L
Derivation of some useful tensor integrals

The first integral we compute is
\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)d\Omega
. Note that the result after performing the integration is a vector, whose
k
-th element may be written as

\left[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)d\Omega\right]_{k}=\sum_{j=1}^{D}A_{j}\int\sigma_{k}\sigma_{j}~d\Omega=\sum_{j=1}^{D}A_{j}\bigg[\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D}\delta_{kj}\bigg],

(247)

where in the last step, we have used Eq. (
246
). Performing the summation over
j
, we straightforwardly obtain
\left[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)d\Omega\right]_{k}=(\Omega_{D}/D)A_{k}
. Hence, we have

\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)d\Omega=\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D}\vec{\mathbf{A}}.

(248)

The second integral we compute is
\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)d\Omega
. Focusing on the
k
-th component, we obtain

\bigg[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)d\Omega\bigg]_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}B_{ij}\int\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}~d\Omega=0,

(249)

where in the last step, we have used Eq. (
246
). Hence, we obtain

\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)d\Omega=0.

(250)

The third integral we compute is
\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)^{2}~d\Omega
. Focusing on the
k
-th component, we obtain

\bigg[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)^{2}d\Omega\bigg]_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}A_{i}A_{j}\int\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}~d\Omega=0,

(251)

where in the last step, we have used Eq. (
246
). Hence, we obtain

\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)^{2}d\Omega=0.

(252)

The fourth integral we compute is
\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)^{2}~d\Omega
. Focusing on the
k
-th component, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigg[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)^{2}d\Omega\bigg]_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}\sum_{l=1}^{D}\sum_{m=1}^{D}B_{ij}B_{lm}\int\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{l}\sigma_{m}\sigma_{k}~d\Omega=0,

(253)

where in the last step, we have used Eq. (
246
). Hence, we obtain

\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)^{2}d\Omega=0.

(254)

The fifth integral we compute is
\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)~d\Omega
. Focusing on the
k
-th component, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigg[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)d\Omega\bigg]_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}\sum_{m=1}^{D}A_{m}B_{ij}\int\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{m}\sigma_{k}~d\Omega=\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}\sum_{m=1}^{D}A_{m}B_{ij}

\displaystyle\times\bigg[\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)}\bigg(\delta_{ij}\delta_{mk}+\delta_{im}\delta_{jk}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jm}\bigg)\bigg],

(255)

where in the last step we have used Eq. (
246
). Summing over the Kronecker deltas and using the fact that
\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathbf{B}\big]=0
, we obtain

\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)d\Omega=\frac{2\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)}\mathbf{B}\vec{\mathbf{A}}.

(256)

The sixth integral we compute is
\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)~d\Omega
. Focusing on the
k
-th component, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigg[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)~d\Omega\bigg]_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}A_{i}B_{ij}\int\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}~d\Omega

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}A_{i}B_{ij}\bigg[\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D}\delta_{jk}\bigg]=\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D}\sum_{i=1}^{D}A_{i}B_{ik}.

(257)

Since,
\mathbf{B}
is a symmetric matrix, we may write
\big[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)~d\Omega\big]_{k}=(\Omega_{D}/D)\sum_{i=1}^{D}B_{ki}A_{i}=(\Omega_{D}/D)\big[\mathbf{B}\vec{\mathbf{A}}\big]_{k}
. Hence, we obtain

\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)~d\Omega=\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D}\mathbf{B}\vec{\mathbf{A}}.

(258)

The seventh integral we compute is
\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)d\Omega
. Focusing on the
mn
-th component, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigg[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)d\Omega\bigg]_{mn}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}B_{ij}\int\sigma_{m}\sigma_{n}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}~d\Omega.=\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}B_{ij}

\displaystyle\times\bigg[\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)}\bigg(\delta_{mn}\delta_{ij}+\delta_{mi}\delta_{nj}+\delta_{mj}\delta_{ni}\bigg)\bigg],

(259)

where in the last step, we have used Eq. (
246
). Summing over the Kronecker deltas, we finally obtain

\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)d\Omega=\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)}\bigg[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathbf{B}\big]\mathbf{I}_{D}+2\mathbf{B}\bigg].

(260)

Using the traceless property of
\mathbf{B}
, we may simplify Eq. (
260
) into

\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)d\Omega=\frac{2\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)}\mathbf{B}.

(261)

Replacing
\mathbf{B}
by
\mathbf{B}^{2}
and
\mathbf{B}^{3}
in Eq. (
260
), we get

\displaystyle\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}^{2}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)d\Omega

\displaystyle=\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)}\bigg[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathbf{B}^{2}\big]\mathbf{I}_{D}+2\mathbf{B}^{2}\bigg],

(262)

\displaystyle\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}^{3}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)d\Omega

\displaystyle=\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)}\bigg[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathbf{B}^{3}\big]\mathbf{I}_{D}+2\mathbf{B}^{3}\bigg].

(263)

The eighth integral we compute is
\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)^{2}d\Omega
. Focusing on the
mn
-th component, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigg[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)^{2}d\Omega\bigg]_{mn}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}\sum_{k=1}^{D}\sum_{l=1}^{D}B_{ij}B_{kl}\int\sigma_{m}\sigma_{n}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}\sigma_{l}~d\Omega.

(264)

We now use Eq. (
246
) to compute the integral. Due to the traceless property of
\mathbf{B}
, any term containing
\delta_{ij}
or
\delta_{kl}
will not contribute after the summation. Considering only those terms which will contribute, we may write Eq. (
264
) as

\displaystyle\bigg[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)^{2}d\Omega\bigg]_{mn}=\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)(D+4)}\sum_{i=1}^{D}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{D}\sum_{k=1}^{D}\sum_{l=1}^{D}B_{ij}B_{kl}\bigg[\delta_{mn}\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{mn}\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}+\delta_{mi}\delta_{nk}\delta_{jl}

\displaystyle+\delta_{mi}\delta_{nl}\delta_{jk}+\delta_{mj}\delta_{nk}\delta_{il}+\delta_{mj}\delta_{nl}\delta_{ik}+\delta_{mk}\delta_{ni}\delta_{jl}

\displaystyle+\delta_{mk}\delta_{nj}\delta_{il}+\delta_{ml}\delta_{ni}\delta_{jk}+\delta_{ml}\delta_{nj}\delta_{ik}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)(D+4)}\bigg[2\delta_{mn}\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}B^{2}_{ij}+8\big[\mathbf{B}^{2}\big]_{mn}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)(D+4)}\bigg[2\delta_{mn}\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathbf{B}^{2}\big]+8\big[\mathbf{B}^{2}\big]_{mn}\bigg].

(265)

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)^{2}d\Omega

\displaystyle=\frac{2\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)(D+4)}\bigg[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathbf{B}^{2}\big]\mathbf{I}_{D}+4\mathbf{B}^{2}\bigg].

(266)

The ninth integral we compute is
\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)\big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}^{2}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)d\Omega
. Focusing on the
mn
-th component, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigg[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}^{2}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)d\Omega\bigg]_{mn}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}\sum_{k=1}^{D}\sum_{l=1}^{D}B_{ij}\big[\mathbf{B}\big]_{kl}\int\sigma_{m}\sigma_{n}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}\sigma_{l}~d\Omega.

(267)

We now use Eq. (
246
) to compute the integral. Due to the traceless property of
\mathbf{B}
, any term containing
\delta_{ij}
will not contribute after the summation. Considering only those terms which will contribute, we may write Eq. (
267
) as

\displaystyle\bigg[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}^{2}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)d\Omega\bigg]_{mn}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)(D+4)}\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}\sum_{k=1}^{D}\sum_{l=1}^{D}B_{ij}\big[\mathbf{B}^{2}\big]_{kl}

\displaystyle\times\bigg[\delta_{mn}\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{mn}\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}+\delta_{mi}\delta_{nj}\delta_{kl}+\delta_{mi}\delta_{nk}\delta_{jl}

\displaystyle+\delta_{mi}\delta_{nl}\delta_{jk}+\delta_{mj}\delta_{ni}\delta_{kl}+\delta_{mj}\delta_{nk}\delta_{il}+\delta_{mj}\delta_{nl}\delta_{ik}

\displaystyle+\delta_{mk}\delta_{ni}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{mk}\delta_{nj}\delta_{il}+\delta_{ml}\delta_{ni}\delta_{jk}+\delta_{ml}\delta_{nj}\delta_{ik}\bigg].

(268)

Summing over the Kronecker deltas, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigg[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}^{2}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)d\Omega\bigg]_{mn}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)(D+4)}\bigg[2\delta_{mn}\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}B_{ij}\big[\mathbf{B}^{2}\big]_{ji}

\displaystyle+2B_{mn}\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathbf{B}^{2}\big]+8\big[\mathbf{B}^{3}\big]_{mn}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{2\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)(D+4)}\bigg[\delta_{mn}\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathbf{B}^{3}\big]+B_{mn}\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathbf{B}^{2}\big]

\displaystyle+4\big[\mathbf{B}^{3}\big]_{mn}\bigg].

(269)

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}^{2}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)d\Omega=\frac{2\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)(D+4)}

\displaystyle\times\bigg[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathbf{B}^{3}\big]\mathbf{I}_{D}+\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathbf{B}^{2}\big]\mathbf{B}+4\mathbf{B}^{3}\bigg].

(270)

The tenth integral we compute is
\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\big)^{3}d\Omega
. Focusing on the
mn
-th component, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigg[\int\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\otimes\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big(\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\cdot\mathbf{B}\vec{\bm{\sigma}}\Big)^{3}d\Omega\bigg]_{mn}=\sum_{g=1}^{D}\sum_{h=1}^{D}\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}\sum_{k=1}^{D}\sum_{l=1}^{D}

\displaystyle B_{gh}B_{ij}B_{kl}\int\sigma_{m}\sigma_{n}\sigma_{g}\sigma_{h}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}\sigma_{l}~d\Omega.

(271)

From (
246
) we have

\displaystyle\int\sigma_{m}\sigma_{n}\sigma_{g}\sigma_{h}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}\sigma_{l}~d\Omega

\displaystyle=\frac{\Omega_{D}}{D(D+2)(D+4)(D+6)}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\{\mathrm{pairings}\,P\}}\delta_{i_{a}i_{b}}\delta_{i_{c}i_{d}}\delta_{i_{e}i_{f}}\delta_{i_{o}i_{p}}.

(272)

Total number of such pairs is
8!/(2^{4}4!)=105
. Since it is tedious to write down all the combinations of the Kronecker deltas, we will resort to a diagrammatic method to compute the integral.

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{16.35916pt}{-16.04909pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
